Title: PerMIT: Securing Persistent Memory for LLM Agents via Machine-Checked Integrity Theorems and Scalable Community Trust

URL Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.28987v1

Published Time: Thu, 22 Oct 2026 00:28:52 GMT

Number of Pages: 146

Markdown Content:
# PerMIT: Securing Persistent Memory for LLM Agents via Machine-Checked Integrity Theorems and Scalable Community Trust 

Tanqiu Jiang 1* Min Bai 2 Sadat Shahriar 2 Wanyu Du 2 Sandesh Swamy 21Stony Brook University 

> 2

AWS AI Labs 

PREPRINT 

ABSTRACT 

Persistent memory is a largely undefended surface for injection and poisoning, and the same risk limits memory sharing among agents. We present PerMIT, a memory architecture with provable integrity guarantees under an explicit trust assumption: only user-typed inputs and values confirmed at a gate are trusted. PerMIT mediates the release and use of untrusted environment-derived values through a finite memory interface, while preserving agent-driven multi-turn planning and tool choice. Over this finite interface, we prove two integrity theorems that are also machine-checked in Lean 4, which together ensure that environment-derived memory reaches a sensitive action only if confirmed at the gate and influences the agent’s behavior only through what the gate displays. Every attack in our threat model thereby reduces to a single auditable decision, which a human, an LLM verifier, or both can make in practice. On Trojan Hippo, PerMIT records zero observed attack success with self-verification and lightweight verifiers. Because each confirmation is a logged verdict on exact content, endorsements accumulate across users, and sufficiently endorsed items become community-confirmed with no further verification required. Under explicit assumptions, we prove and corroborate in simulation a co-scaling property: as the community grows, the risks of promoting poison and of wrongly evicting benign knowledge fall, while per-user verification effort does not rise. On τ 2-bench, communities of up to 90 autonomous agents use PerMIT to build shared knowledge bases with no direct memory injection promoted, and the resulting knowledge significantly improves new agents on held-out tasks, suggesting a path toward agents that safely self-improve at test time.

# Part I 

# Main Paper 

1 Introduction 

By carrying preferences, decisions, and hard-won context across sessions, persistent memory transforms a stateless LLM into an agent that becomes more capable and personalized with continued use. Although agents benefit from reading and writing persistent memory, this capability also creates an attack surface that the underlying models are poorly equipped to defend. Poisoned information can readily enter persistent memory when an agent browses webpages or invokes tools containing planted injections (Das et al., 2026). Such attacks can turn a one-shot prompt injection (Debenedetti et al., 2024) into a persistent compromise, and a single compromise could easily propagate to a broader community if that memory were ever shared. Existing memory-sharing frameworks such as the Open Knowledge Format (McVeety & Hormati, 2026) (OKF) define formal protocols for writing and exchanging knowledge among agents but do not address memory attacks. This difficult-to-control risk has limited the practical deployment of memory sharing between agents. Existing defenses validate retrieved content, govern access, or enforce information-flow policies during agent execution. A-MemGuard empirically detects context-dependent poisoning through consensus among memory-induced reasoning paths (Wei et al., 2025), whereas Collaborative Memory enforces dynamic read/write policies over provenance-tagged private and shared memory fragments (Rezazadeh et al., 2025). However, neither approach provides formal security guarantees against malicious memories. Beyond memory-specific defenses, CaMeL addresses prompt injection during agent execution through isolated planning with capability-based policies and user confirmation (Debenedetti et al., 2025); FIDES formalizes IFC guarantees and supports selective hiding and constrained inspection of tool results (Costa et al., 2025). PerMIT builds on this isolation-and-mediation lineage to secure the persistent-memory lifecycle. PerMIT enforces its trust boundary at the memory interface. The harness captures environment-derived content, including tool outputs, as untrusted and mediates its release and use. The protected agent chooses its next operation and can re-plan on endorsed feedback, so its internal reasoning is not inspected or taint-tracked. The noninterference proofs quantify over arbitrary agent strategies using this finite interface (Goguen & Meseguer, 1982). However, preventing all influence from untrusted memory would also prevent legitimate tasks whose next steps depend on information recalled from that memory. To mitigate the safety–utility tradeoff, PerMIT adopts endorsement (Myers et al., 2006), a formal relaxation that promotes an untrusted value to trusted status only after the user confirms the exact value displayed at the gate, so untrusted memory can be selectively endorsed and used rather than barred. Using this finite mediation surface and endorsement mechanism, we prove Action Integrity as an exact authorization property and Reasoning Integrity as noninterference modulo the display transcript (Sabelfeld & Myers, 2004; Askarov & Sabelfeld, 2007), and machine-check their single-user core in Lean 4 1. These machine-checked guarantees hold over unbounded interface-conformant executions (§2.2), separating enforced mediation from the correctness of individual endorsement decisions. Endorsement preserves benign utility by allowing external values to become trusted through an interface with a finite set of operations. The gate displays the exact value, original user instruction, and source, making the decision auditable. On Trojan Hippo, PerMIT combines live agent rollouts with targeted action-boundary probes; self-verification and lightweight verifiers record zero observed ASR while retaining useful memory capabilities (Table 1). Although security concerns have limited the deployment of shared memory, Karpathy’s LLM-Wiki (Karpathy, 2026) illustrates its potential: thousands of users adopted his shared coding-agent setup to improve their  

> 1The Lean 4 artifact is available upon request and will be released with the code when the paper is published. Its scope and correspondence are documented in Appendix E and Supplement S9.
> 3PerMIT: Securing Persistent Memory for LLM Agents PREPRINT

agents’ performance. LLM-Wiki’s success was an exception because the author is highly reputable, and the shared knowledge had accumulated thousands of public endorsements through GitHub stars. However, scaling shared memory to diverse communities requires accepting contributions from ordinary users and agents, allowing adversaries to pose as legitimate contributors and spread harmful content. To solve this dilemma, PerMIT learns from the success of LLM-Wiki and allows each shared knowledge item to accumulate trust independently, paralleling security mechanisms in distributed systems that aggregate endorsements over shared objects (Walsh & Sirer, 2006). Each logged confirmation is bound to the exact content, allowing verdicts from different users and agents to accumulate as item-level trust. Each item requires confirmation until its accumulated trust reaches the “Community Confirmed” threshold. As the population grows, confirmations and endorsements allow more items to reach this status and become directly available to new users without further verification, so safety and convenience improve together. Under explicit assumptions, we prove that poison-promotion and wrongful-eviction risks decrease as the community grows, while the average per-user confirmation burden does not grow (Supplementary Theorem S6.13). Our simulation spanning five billion promotion attempts also corroborates this co-scaling behavior. PerMIT thus supplies the security assurance missing from the Open Knowledge Format (McVeety & Hormati, 2026) while remaining compatible with its knowledge-sharing protocol. Finally, we close the loop by using PerMIT to construct community-confirmed shared knowledge bases on 

τ 2-bench (Barres et al., 2025) with up to 90 fully autonomous agents and no human involvement. None of the tested direct injection payloads reaches the Community-Confirmed tier, and a zero-state agent (i.e. a fresh agent with no prior context or memory) connected to the resulting knowledge base significantly outperforms an agent without shared knowledge on held-out, experience-dependent telecom tasks (§5). In summary, this paper makes three contributions: 1. Securing agent memory with machine-checked integrity. 

PerMIT mediates a finite memory interface and binds each endorsement to an exact displayed value and its intended use. Under explicit trust assumptions, PerMIT provides two integrity guarantees, machine-checked in Lean 4 for the single-user core, and an auditable confirmation gate for human or automated verification. 2. Shared memory in which safety and convenience scale together rather than trade off. Because each gate decision is a logged verdict on exact content, verdicts accumulate into community-confirmed knowledge, which is the layer that existing memory-sharing frameworks such as OKF lack. Under explicit assumptions, we prove and corroborate in simulation that poison-promotion and wrongful-eviction risks fall as the community grows while per-user verification effort does not rise. 3. Safe, autonomous self-construction of shared knowledge. Our experiments show that autonomous agent communities can safely construct useful knowledge bases with PerMIT. By enabling agents to generate, verify, and certify knowledge in complex environments, PerMIT provides a concrete path toward test-time learning and self-evolving agents. 

2 Background and Related Work 

2.1 Persistent Memory as an Attack Surface 

Persistent-memory architectures manage context across sessions through tiered storage, consolidation, and revision (Packer et al., 2023; Chhikara et al., 2025; Xu et al., 2025). Because stored observations later return to the agent’s context, transient exposure can become durable influence. Attacks enter through direct poisoning of a memory or knowledge base (Chen et al., 2024), query-only interaction (Dong et al., 2025), or indirect ingestion of retrieved artifacts and webpages (Srivastava & He, 2025; Zou et al., 2026; Yang et al., 2026b); shared state can also contaminate users without an adversary (Yang et al., 2026a). The defining risk is temporal: Trojan Hippo plants a dormant payload through one untrusted tool call and activates it only in a later session (Das et al., 2026). This persistence makes turn-local prompt-injection defences insufficient and motivates guarantees over the memory lifecycle (Lin et al., 2026).  

> 4PerMIT: Securing Persistent Memory for LLM Agents PREPRINT

2.2 Defenses and Formal Coverage 

Existing defenses draw their boundaries at different layers. Content-based methods detect anomalous memories or task drift (Wei et al., 2025; Abdelnabi et al., 2024). Isolation and privilege-control methods constrain how untrusted information can influence tool interactions (Wen et al., 2026; Shi et al., 2025). TMA-NM (Louck, 2026) binds memory authority to origin and studies consequential-action safety using TLA+ model checking and an inductive-invariant argument. Willison’s Dual LLM pattern introduced privileged planning, quarantined processing, and opaque references (Willison, 2023). CaMeL adds capability-based enforcement and user confirmation, while leaving formal verification as future work (Debenedetti et al., 2025, §§2,5,10). AgentDyn reports zero utility for its CaMeL configurations under automated dynamic-task evaluation (Li et al., 2026). FIDES provides a formal IFC model with integrity noninterference, selective variable hiding, and schema-constrained quarantined inspection (Costa et al., 2025). Its type-aware policies can permit bounded influence; PerMIT centers exact-value, tool-scoped endorsement and the complete display transcript. Whereas CaMeL and FIDES address prompt injection in agent execution more generally, our contribution centers the persistent-memory lifecycle, with Action Integrity and Reasoning Integrity guarantees for arbitrarily long interactions through the memory interface under explicit trust assumptions. We machine-check the integrity theorems in Lean 4 and aggregate item-level endorsements in the federated tier. Planning and tool choice remain agent-driven, including replanning on endorsed feedback. 

2.3 Shared Memory and Evaluation 

Shared-memory work has primarily addressed exchange, access, and portability. OKF standardizes knowl-edge exchange (McVeety & Hormati, 2026), Collaborative Memory enforces dynamic policies over private and shared fragments (Rezazadeh et al., 2025), and Portable Agent Memory proposes cryptographically verified transfer and injection-resistant rehydration (Ravindran, 2026). These mechanisms complement PerMIT but do not establish recipient-side action and reasoning integrity for adopted content. General agent-security benchmarks emphasize prompt injection within a task episode (Debenedetti et al., 2024; Li et al., 2026; Weng et al., 2026); Trojan Hippo most directly matches our cross-session threat model (Das et al., 2026). We therefore use Trojan Hippo to evaluate persistent-memory security and τ 2-bench (Barres et al., 2025) to evaluate the end-to-end utility of autonomously constructed shared knowledge. 

3 The PerMIT Framework 

3.1 Threat Model 

PerMIT assumes an adversary capable of controlling or manipulating content obtained from the external environment, including tool outputs, web content, external files and codebases. Poisoned payloads may enter persistent memory when the agent invokes tools, browses the web, or inspects external files or code. PerMIT establishes a provenance-based trust boundary: only values typed in directly by the user or exact values explicitly confirmed by the user through the confirmation interface are treated as trusted. All other environment-derived content remains untrusted and cannot reach the protected LLM unless it first passes through this confirmation gate and gets verified to be trusted. 

3.2 The Single-User Tier: a Finite Memory Interface 

The P-LLM remains an agent that chooses its next operation and revises its plan using endorsed feedback. Prompts describe the interface; the harness enforces its observation and flow rules without inspecting the agent’s internal reasoning. Gate decisions control which values are endorsed; the harness enforces how values cross the interface. Figure 1 summarizes PerMIT’s architecture, trust boundary, and shared-memory trust-accumulation mechanism. Architecturally, PerMIT places a Protected LLM agent ( P-LLM ) on the protected side of the trust boundary and uses a Quarantined Reader ( Q-LLM ) to inspect and retrieve relevant content from untrusted memory. The Q-LLM can read the memory store but has no tool capabilities. In

Figure 1: PerMIT mediates observations and data flows at the persistent-memory interface while leaving planning and tool choice to the protected agent. our implementation, it is realized as a recursive language model (RLM) (Zhang et al., 2025), enabling it to navigate stores larger than a context window without loading their contents into the P-LLM’s trusted context (implementation details in Section B.4). Untrusted content is exposed to the P-LLM only through opaque handles rather than as readable bytes, and the content can be released only after endorsement through the confirmation gate (Lemma D.1). The P-LLM interacts with persistent memory through a fixed algebra of seven operations: write , recall , fill , crystallize , display , confirm , and evict . In particular, recall returns readable bytes only for trusted content and otherwise returns an opaque handle; fill searches the untrusted region of memory and likewise returns handles; and crystallize produces a schema-bound summary that remains untrusted when written back to memory. Section B explains all seven operations from a systems perspective, with a worked poisoning trace. The full interface also mediates tool invocation, trusted retrieval, input channels, and batched iteration (Section D.1). Sensitive-tool literals must byte-match typed or tool-endorsed content to bypass the gate; handles always gate. Data tools may receive agent-composed bytes. Trust denotes provenance and authorization, not factual correctness. PerMIT’s finite memory interface yields two guarantees, stated compactly below. Both assume source-based labels, immutable stored blocks, and faithful displays; Appendices D.2.1 and D.2.2 summarize the arguments; Supplement S3 gives the full proofs. 

Action Integrity (Theorem D.3, compact form). Assume trusted endorsement: acceptance authorizes the exact displayed bytes for the named tool. For every execution from a well-formed initial state, under any attack, each argument reaching a sensitive tool is either an exact user-typed value or a value endorsed by that user at its exact bytes for that exact tool, at or before the call. Endorsement grants reusable authority for an 

6PerMIT: Securing Persistent Memory for LLM Agents PREPRINT 

exact value and tool. It does not bind argument position, task, purpose, time, or the other arguments of a call (Appendix D.2.1). 

Reasoning Integrity modulo the display transcript (Theorem D.5, compact form). Consider any two executions ρ1, ρ 2 from matched initial configurations (the same protected view, interaction history, and logs), with identical user-typed inputs, coupled randomness, the same schedule, and a common horizon. Their untrusted content may differ arbitrarily. Then 

DT (ρ1) = DT (ρ2) =⇒ Beh P (ρ1) = Beh P (ρ2).

Here DT is the complete display transcript, including previews, decision panes, and decisions; Beh P is the sequence of P-LLM operations, tool invocations, and replies. Thus hidden untrusted content has no additional influence once the display transcript is fixed. A never-endorsed, untrusted-derived value cannot be exfiltrated through a URL, search query, or file path without an accepting confirmation over that exact value at that exact tool in that call (Corollary D.4). This protection applies to information that has never been confirmed. Once a value has been confirmed for a particular tool, PerMIT allows the agent to recall it and reuse the exact value directly with that tool without repeating the confirmation. Likewise, an attacker cannot alter the P-LLM’s operations, tool invocations, or ungated sink flows without producing a corresponding difference in the complete user-facing display transcript (Corollary D.6). Within this threat model, every attempt to release untrusted content across the trust boundary is therefore reduced to a single auditable event: the confirmation decision. As such, PerMIT isolates the risks posed by untrusted memory. Its safety guarantees now rest on an explicit trust assumption: any value approved at the gate is treated as trusted. By mediating all memory risk at this single decision point, the confirmation gate becomes a common verification interface that can be operated by the human user, an LLM verifier, or both. 

3.3 The Federated Tier 

The federated tier extends the single-user confirmation mechanism to shared memory, allowing users to build on one another’s endorsements instead of independently reviewing every entry. Because every confirmation is a signed and logged decision on exact content for an exact tool use, it serves two purposes: releasing the content for the confirming user and depositing an endorsement on the entry itself. For a shared entry, these endorsements accumulate across users, with each identity contributing at most one endorsement to a given retrieved memory entry. Once an entry collects at least N fresh endorsements for a tool, it is promoted to the “community-confirmed" tier. Subsequent users may then use the entry without repeating the confirmation process. Because honest verifiers can make mistakes and adversaries may reject indiscriminately, treating every rejection as grounds for immediate deletion would create a denial-of-service attack surface against benign knowledge in the shared memory. PerMIT instead handles disputed entries through a demand-paced panel. A qualifying rejection does not immediately delete an entry. Instead, it freezes promotion, visibly flags the entry, and routes the dispute to an auditable panel process. The entry remains retrievable and may still be used after personal confirmation. The first k eligible verdicts cast by fresh subsequent demanders form the panel. If rejecting verdicts reach the rejection threshold mrej , the entry is evicted from the shared candidate tier. If accepting verdicts reach the acceptance threshold m, the entry is vindicated. Together with rejection-rate limits and rejecter accountability, this mechanism tolerates isolated honest mistakes while containing adversaries that issue systematic false rejections. This construction makes the safety–utility trade-off quantitatively explicit. PerMIT bounds poison-promotion and wrongful-eviction risks, and models confirmation labor, as functions of verifier accuracy, adversarial capacity, the endorsement quorum, and the panel configuration. A poisoned entry can reach the community-confirmed tier through only two routes: the ordinary endorsement path without a qualifying rejection, or a path that resumes after panel vindication. The parameters qpre 

and qpan lower-bound the conditional probabilities that an honest reviewer detects poison at the ordinary gate and on the panel, respectively. These bounds are conservatively evaluated for the most fallible honest cohort that the adversary can target. They hold uniformly over adaptive presentation, stopping, and re-entry strategies, regardless of how many users encounter the entry. Under the illustrative simulation setting specified in Appendix A.1, the expression evaluates to Batt = 8 .96 × 10 −3, an analytical ceiling of 0.896%

.Under the specified demand, coverage, and threshold schedules, poison-promotion and wrongful-eviction risks decrease as the community grows, while the average per-user confirmation burden for covered benign shared demand does not grow. Required user approvals are capped at one per user, value, and tool when endorsed copies are retained. Personal demand and nonconforming shared demand retain their stated residual costs (simulations: Section 4.2). Formal statements and proofs are given in Supplementary Sections S5–S6. Sharing can improve safety and convenience together because later users reuse earlier endorsements. Both simulations (Section 4.2) and live tests (Section 5) provide complementary empirical support: the former characterize the safety–effort trade-off, while the latter demonstrate growing reusable knowledge with roughly level per-agent review effort. 

4 Results 

We evaluate single-user security and utility on Trojan Hippo (§4.1) and test federated-tier certified ceilings against adaptive protocol adversaries (§4.2). 

4.1 The Single-User Tier Setup. We evaluate four backbones across Trojan Hippo memory and defense configurations (Table 1; Das et al., 2026). PerMIT’s live containment harness places poison in untrusted persistent memory and combines a subsequent agent rollout with a targeted reader-to-tool probe. OpenGate accepts every confirmation request; OracleGate models a user who always makes the correct decision; LLMVerifier makes the decision with a language model. We instantiate LLMVerifier using self-verification and two lightweight Qwen models. Utility reports arithmetic and harmonic means (AM/HM) over seven capability axes, with HM primary. Appendix A.2 details the protocol and counting; Appendix A.5 reports benign FPR. 

Existing defenses do not provide consistent protection. Every baseline has at least one model–backend configuration with nonzero ASR. Capability-restricting defenses reduce exposure by disabling functionality required by benign tasks, frequently collapsing HM, whereas prompt-level and retrieval-time defenses preserve more functionality but continue to admit attacks in other settings. 

PerMIT structurally closes the ungated memory-injection path. Under the formal model, untrusted memory cannot reach the protected agent as readable content without endorsement (Lemma D.1), confining memory-mediated attacks to an auditable interface (Corollary D.6). Our live probes test this interface after

4.2 The Federated Tier 

We test the federated analysis with five billion simulated promotion attempts at the geometry of Section 3.3 and a 120-user community sharing 122 entries, including two poisoned entries. Simulations use controlled reviewer responses with poison catch rate q and benign acceptance rate pb = 0 .9; the correlation sweep varies verdict dependence. Appendix A.1 gives settings and additional figures.

Figure 2(b) shows that correlated reviewer errors raise risk, which argues for drawing reviewers from diverse models and users. Figure 2(c) justifies the panel setting: for a 31-seat panel, an acceptance threshold of m=20 with the complementary rejection threshold mrej =12 keeps both poisoned vindication and wrongful eviction below the 10 −2 design target. We then attacked the simulated protocol and measured how often a poisoned entry is actually promoted. For every tested number of honest users shown a poisoned entry, the observed promotion rate remains below the theoretical bound (Figure 3), and none of the adaptive attack strategies we simulated exceeds the bound (Figure 4). 

Safety with less verification effort. In a 120-user community (Figure 5), reusing community-confirmed entries saves each user 2.36 required approvals on average ( 38% ) compared with verifying alone, and a larger quorum provides substantial safety gains at a modest cost: raising N from 3 to 14 reduces the certified risk bound by a factor of 58 while adding only 0.64 required approvals per user. 

5 Self-Constructing the Knowledge Base 

We test autonomous shared-knowledge construction on τ 2-bench telecom (Barres et al., 2025), with one construction run per model and community size M ∈ { 12 , 24 , 60 , 90 }. A single model family explores tasks, verifies extracted knowledge, and reviews shared entries. Publication follows safety verification, sharability screening, and consent to the exact content. The protocol uses a promotion quorum N =10 and fixed dispute thresholds k=31 , m=20 , and mrej =12 .To demonstrate the safety performance through PerMIT, we manually submitted direct memory-injection payloads in crafted entries through τ 2-bench’s normal submission path. None of the 12 direct memory-injection payloads reached the Community-Confirmed tier.

Utility uses 24 held-out tasks with two trials each and matched seeds (48 paired episodes per arm). Across M =12 , 24 , 60 , 90 , the retained knowledge bases contain 3–7, 7–12, 30–40, and 45–64 entries respectively, across the three backbones. Review effort per agent remains roughly level from 24 to 90 agents as the shared stock grows. This supports the practical co-scaling pattern of more reusable knowledge without proportional growth in individual review effort. At M =90 , all means exceed the knowledge-free baseline; pooled gains across community sizes survive three-backbone Holm correction for Sonnet and Kimi, but not GLM-5. Kimi-built knowledge also improves a zero-state Claude-Haiku-4.5 agent by +0 .198 [+0 .073 , +0 .333] in a separate 400-step, 48-episode evaluation (details in Appendix A.7). Results for the banking domain, and an analysis of when shared knowledge does and does not add utility, are in Appendix A.6. 

6 Conclusion and Future Directions 

PerMIT contributes a finite memory interface with machine-checked integrity guarantees, a federated protocol that turns exact-content endorsements into reusable trust, and an autonomous workflow for constructing useful shared knowledge. Under explicit assumptions, the protocol bounds poison promotion and wrongful eviction while reducing repeated verification. The agent communities promoted no direct injection payloads and improved held-out task performance through PerMIT. These results motivate three future directions. First, provenance and endorsement do not ensure usefulness: shared memory may contain redundant, inaccurate, or hallucinated knowledge, motivating quality-aware gates and active memory management. Second, estimating rare promotion events required five billion simulated attempts and over 50 hours of computation; more efficient calibration with live verifiers and users would strengthen deployment evidence. Third, adaptive payload search against live verifiers remains an open empirical question. Our simulations test the protocol’s conditional safety boundaries through adaptive protocol attacks under controlled reviewer responses; they do not replace adaptive payload search against live verifiers. Human or agent memory managers could combine memories and review their joint meaning and intended use, then seek fresh user endorsements of the combined content before it becomes trusted. Testing this workflow against adaptive endorsement and delayed-combination attacks is a future direction. Matched component controls and measured human review effort would further clarify deployment trade-offs.

We are especially grateful for his suggestions during research meetings and for his thoughtful comments during the preparation and revision of the manuscript. A.2 The Trojan Hippo Benchmark in Brief 

Trojan Hippo (Das et al., 2026) evaluates persistent memory poisoning under an indirect-injection threat model: the user is trusted, the attacker controls only content the agent reads through its tools, and the attacker’s goal is to exfiltrate sensitive user data to an attacker-controlled address. The attack has two stages. In the injection stage, a crafted inbound email read through a mailbox tool carries a payload that the memory backend silently stores; in the activation stage, after N unrelated benign sessions, a user request on a sensitive topic triggers the stored payload, which induces the agent to send the user’s message to the attacker through an outbound tool. Table 3 summarizes the benchmark’s coverage and scale. 

PerMIT live containment protocol. PerMIT replaces the benchmark memory backend with its RLM reader and confirmation interface. The four main-table backbones are served through AWS Bedrock. Each case is placed in a fresh store as untrusted email-tool output in an ingestion session and exercised from a

The harness combines a live protected-agent rollout with a targeted reader-to-tool probe: the reader fills an address field, and the probe attempts to pass the resulting handle to send_email ,with an additional literal-address variant. This explicitly attempts to exercise the action boundary without relying solely on the planner to initiate exfiltration. A case succeeds if either executed probe produces a recorded sink invocation whose serialized arguments contain the benchmark attacker address. The probes run sequentially in the same per-case store. In LLMVerifier mode, the reader probe also performs retrieval-stage crystallization and confirmation. OpenGate accepts every confirmation; OracleGate supplies the idealized correct-user decisions; LLMVerifier uses the designated model. The comparisons are between complete configurations. 

Audit baselines. Our A-MemGuard and LLM-Auditor reimplementations share the benchmark retrieval path and reuse the corresponding undefended cases. A-MemGuard cross-checks per-record reasoning chains and maintains a per-case lesson store; LLM-Auditor independently audits each retrieved record. Retrieval depth is K=8 for RAG and K=10 for Mem0. Both discard flagged records; our auditor is a drop-only variant, not the rewriting auditor described in the original work. 

A.3 Implementation Gap in the Provable-Policy Baseline 

In the supplied Trojan Hippo implementation, GLM-5 records 10% ASR under the provable-policy baseline with both Explicit memory and Mem0 (two successful attacks among twenty cases per configuration). The successful cases are health/01 and legal/03 for Explicit, and finance/01 and legal/03 for Mem0. Our code audit identified inconsistent taint propagation in search_emails : the tool marks the session untrusted in SessionTrustManager but does not update the separate ProvablePolicyManager state consulted by send_email . An untrusted search can therefore leave the outbound guard’s trusted state unchanged. All four successful traces exhibit the corresponding inconsistency: an untrusted-read notification is followed by an outbound check with is_trusted=True and will_block=False , and the benchmark’s

(b) Safety–effort frontier at central overlap for qpre ∈ { 0.2, 0.4, 0.6} (decoupled qpan = 0 .6): raising N from 3 to 14 costs 0.64 required approvals per user and buys a 58 × lower certified ceiling; no quorum reaches below the panel-leak floor 

ppanel = 8 .3 × 10 −3, and moving between q-curves is not free. outbox-based attack checker reports success. These logs do not establish that the successful sends were triggered by retrieval of poisoned persistent-memory entries. We retain the measured rates as results of the supplied benchmark implementation. The observed failures illustrate a practical enforcement gap: missing taint updates or inconsistent security state can permit attacker-controlled content to induce disclosure despite an intended information-flow restriction. Formal guarantees depend on faithful implementation of their assumptions across all relevant execution paths; these results expose a loophole in the evaluated enforcement rather than a counterexample to the intended policy’s guarantee. 

A.4 Additional Backbones and Serving Configurations 

Three backbones are reported here rather than in Table 1. Qwen-3.6-27B and Gemma-4-31B-it were served locally under vLLM with a common resource configuration (tensor parallelism over four GPUs, 32,768-token context, 0.92 GPU memory utilization, automatic tool choice) and family-specific parsers. Qwen-3.6-27B: 

vllm serve --tensor-parallel-size 4 --max-model-len 32768 --gpu-memory-utilization 0.92 --enable-auto-tool-choice --tool-call-parser qwen3_coder --reasoning-parser qwen3 

Gemma-4-31B-it additionally requires a model-specific chat template and, on our stack, the native sampler: 

VLLM_USE_FLASHINFER_SAMPLER=0 vllm serve --tensor-parallel-size 4 --max-model-len 32768 --gpu-memory-utilization 0.92 --enable-auto-tool-choice --tool-call-parser gemma4 --reasoning-parser gemma4 --chat-template tool_chat_template_gemma4.jinja 

Tool-call parsing on a self-hosted server is part of the measurement rather than a detail of it, because a defence that mediates tool calls is sensitive to how those calls are extracted from the model’s output, and the

parser is necessarily family-specific. The two backbones therefore share a serving stack but not a parser or chat template: they are comparable to each other at the stack level, not to the Bedrock-served set of the main table, and the per-family parser and template choices are recorded rather than left implicit. The lightweight verifiers (Qwen-3.5-2B and Qwen-3.5-4B) were served under the same vLLM stack, one GPU each, with a 16,384-token context, 0.90 GPU memory utilization, the native sampler, and the family’s reasoning parser; verifiers render verdicts rather than tool calls, so no tool-call parser is involved. GPT-5.5 is served through the same Bedrock APIs as GPT-5.4. The pattern of Table 1 survives the change of serving stack. Both self-hosted backbones are highly vulnerable undefended, peaking at 40 per cent attack success on Qwen-3.6-27B and 75 on Gemma-4-31B-it, both on sliding-window context; both zero the harmonic mean under the two capability-removing defences; and both reach 96 .7 AM under PerMIT with the oracle at the gate, with ASR = 0 in every occupant cell. The one instructive difference is on the small verifiers, which are harder on these two backbones than on any Bedrock one. Whether the cause is the serving stack, the family-specific parsing and chat template, or the backbone itself is not identified by these runs, since the three are confounded by construction; the honest statement is that a small external verifier needs validating against the backbone, the parser, and the stack it will actually run behind.

A.5 Detailed Verifier Results

Table 6 extends OracleGate and the three automated configurations to seven backbones, adding benign FPR and available decimal precision; OpenGate is reported separately for the four main-table backbones. Detailed scores use the available recorded precision; overlapping main-table entries are rounded half up from that precision. GPT-5.4 retains the authoritative precision of Table 1. Table 5 summarizes FPR for its four backbones. Three readings the main table compresses are visible here. First, attack success is zero in all twenty-eight cells, which is the only column with no spread at all. Second, verifier size does not order the results: the 4B verifier has the higher harmonic mean on four of the seven backbones and the lower one on three, its false-positive rate is lower on five, and its two directions do not agree, since it wins by 25 .0 points of HM on Qwen-3.6-27B and loses by 17 .0 on Kimi-K2.5. The two are therefore better described as two draws from one quality tier than as a size ladder, and a two-point size difference is not the axis to select on. Third, the backbone matters more than the verifier. For a fixed verifier the spread across backbones reaches 18 .3 points of AM and 45 .1 of HM under the 2B model, while for a fixed backbone the spread across the three model occupants reaches 20 points of AM on Kimi-K2.5. A deployment choosing a small verifier should therefore validate it against its own backbone rather than against a published ranking. 

Reading the security results. ASR summarizes each complete configuration. Reader outputs may already be clean or refuse an attack, so not every case presents harmful bytes to the verifier. Appendix A.2 specifies the live probes and case-counting protocol. 

Gate activity and utility. Table 7 reports calls and rejections across six benign diagnostic suites with 20 checks each. These 120 checks have a different accounting unit from the seven capability axes summarized in Table 1. FPR is rejections divided by calls, and calls per check is calls divided by 120. These totals measure gate activity; they do not measure human review time or the fraction of tasks interrupted. Verifier choice can substantially affect utility. Kimi with Qwen-3.5-4B has HM 50.3, compared with 89.7 under its oracle gate. For GPT-5.4, the authoritative main-table values are oracle AM/HM 89/86 and self-verification 83/79, a decrease of approximately 6 AM and 7 HM points at that precision. Larger verifiers do not consistently have lower FPR. Within PerMIT, these oracle-to-verifier comparisons retain the RLM reader and interface, characterizing the utility cost associated with automated gate decisions relative to correct endorsement. Comparisons against other memory backends instead evaluate complete systems; separating retrieval quality from the overhead of mediation would require matched reader controls. Human review time is a separate deployment measure.

these reference conditions are not directly comparable, the table reports the confirmatory contrast against the knowledge-free baseline only. The self-constructed knowledge base produces no detectable utility gain in banking: all confidence in-tervals include zero. This contrast with telecom is consistent with the information available in the two environments. Telecom tasks require causal, experience-derived knowledge about how the environment behaves—knowledge that is absent from the supplied documentation and can be acquired only through interaction. Banking tasks primarily require locating information in a well-indexed document corpus that the environment’s retrieval system already makes available on demand. Consequently, the telecom community can contribute knowledge that later agents would otherwise lack, whereas the banking community largely distils information that each agent can already retrieve independently. Together with GLM-5’s near-ceiling telecom result, these findings identify a practical boundary on the utility of shared memory: self-constructed knowledge is most valuable when it captures scarce, experience-derived information, and least valuable when the environment already supplies the required information or the underlying agent has little remaining performance headroom. 

A.7 Autonomous Telecom Construction: Methods and Additional Results Construction and evaluation. The construction ladder comprises one run for each of three model families (Claude-Sonnet-4.6, GLM-5, and Kimi-K2.5) and four community sizes M ∈ { 12 , 24 , 60 , 90 }. Each run uses one model family for all construction roles. Agents crystallize session knowledge into an untrusted handle, submit its exact content to the safety gate for reasoning use, screen approved content for generality and privacy, and consent to publication. Other agents then review shared entries.

The standard utility evaluation uses 24 held-out tasks with two trials each (48 task/trial pairs per arm) and a 200-step limit. No Prior Knowledge (A0) provides no shared knowledge base, PerMIT (A2) supplies community-confirmed knowledge, and Expert Manual (A4) uses the benchmark’s alternative hand-written workflow policy. A4 is not the original policy with extra instructions appended, nor an oracle upper bound on task performance. A0 and A2 keep prompts identical outside the knowledge block. A2 includes all retained entries, with a coarse ordering that distinguishes uncontested from vindicated entries, without truncation. Generated experience primarily covers MMS troubleshooting; the reported Sonnet and Kimi gains are concentrated in that task family. This supports transfer of relevant experience, without establishing gains for every task family. 

Pairing and uncertainty. Seeds are matched by task and trial across arms. Table 2 separates the M =90 

means from the analysis pooled across all four A2 community sizes against corresponding A0 episodes. Table 9 gives the complete size ladder and repeats the pooled analysis for reference. Pooling yields 192 paired differences but reuses the evaluation tasks and baseline; these are not 192 independent tasks. The analysis resamples whole tasks jointly across their trials and sizes: 95% confidence intervals use 10,000 cluster-bootstrap resamples, and the continuous-score test uses 20,000 task-cluster sign-flip permutations, both with analysis seed 20260811. Holm correction across the three same-backbone comparisons gives adjusted 

p-values of 0.0066 for Claude-Sonnet-4.6, 0.0325 for Kimi-K2.5, and 0.9356 for GLM-5. The bootstrap confidence intervals are pointwise, not multiplicity-adjusted. Using m=5 as a conservative multiplicity sensitivity analysis gives adjusted p=0 .0110 for Sonnet and p=0 .0650 for Kimi. These quantify evaluation uncertainty for the observed knowledge bases, not construction variability; pooled intervals must not be read as M =90 intervals. Step-limit terminations receive zero environment-completion score; infrastructure failures are excluded from the common paired set and counted separately. 

Fixed geometry and finite review opportunities. The construction ladder keeps N =10 , k=31 , m=20 ,and mrej =12 fixed. The panel can terminate when a decision threshold is reached; 31 votes are not required for every resolution. Nevertheless, M =12 cannot supply 20 fresh accepting panelists, nor the initiating rejection plus 12 further rejecting panelists required for eviction. Entries can be detected and frozen without reaching either terminal outcome. At M =24 , early threshold resolution can be feasible, subject to eligibility.

Construction is sequential within one epoch: an entry published by agent i has at most M − i later reviewers. Raw promotion fractions therefore mix review quality with available demand. From M =24 to 90, reviews per agent change from 13.92 to 14.27 for Sonnet, 9.58 to 7.16 for GLM-5, and 12.25 to 10.70 for Kimi, while evaluated knowledge grows from 7 to 48, 10 to 45, and 12 to 64 entries, respectively. This is a finite-scale knowledge/effort trade-off, not a monotonic task-performance trend or an asymptotic theorem. At matched remaining-review horizon H=30 , benign yield from M =60 to 90 is 0.833 to 0.833, 0.824 to 1.000, and 0.826 to 0.976 for Sonnet, GLM-5, and Kimi, respectively; one run per setting limits inference about population effects. 

Unfiltered shared-knowledge control. The existing A1 arm supplies shared knowledge without the community-promotion filter. Table 10 compares A1 and A2 at the same M =90 construction budget, rather than comparing A1 to the four-size pooled result. A1 has higher mean utility on all three backbones. These are descriptive comparisons: the retained contents and prompt lengths differ, and a paired A1–A2 test is not reported here. This is a benign-utility comparison, not a measurement of relative attack resistance. It establishes that unfiltered shared experience can help; it does not isolate the promotion filter on identical candidate pools or substitute for a simpler voting control. 

Cross-backbone transfer. Claude-Haiku-4.5 uses knowledge built at M =90 by the indicated producer; its simulated user is GLM-5. The 200-step comparisons use 47 common task/trial pairs after one infrastructure failure in the wider Haiku comparison set. Table 11 reports that common set. The separate 400-step Kimi-to-Haiku evaluation uses 24 tasks with two trials each (48 pairs) and yields ∆= + 0 .1979 [+0 .0729 , +0 .3333] .Its 24 task clusters comprise 11 MMS, four mobile-data, and nine service tasks. This result is reported separately because its episode set and step budget differ; its effect is not added to the 200-step baseline to reconstruct an absolute mean. 

Scope of the safety probes. The separate 12-payload direct memory-injection campaign reports no community promotion. This endpoint concerns access to the shared fast path; it does not measure personal acceptance, use before promotion, or every intermediate lifecycle transition. 

B The Memory Interface in Practice 

This appendix restates the memory interface of Section 3.2 from a systems perspective: the seven operations and the fixed alphabet of replies the P-LLM can receive (Section B.1), the label lifecycle (Section B.2), a step-by-step trace of a memory-poisoning episode (Section B.3), and the implementation of the Quarantined Reader (Section B.4). It is informative: no rule reads it and no proof cites it. The normative transition schema 

> 24

PerMIT: Securing Persistent Memory for LLM Agents PREPRINT 

and Rules R1–R11 are in Supplement S2; Section D.1 summarizes their role in the guarantees. Throughout, 

P denotes the P-LLM and Q the Q-LLM. 

B.1 Operations and the reply alphabet 

Every reply the interpreter returns to P is drawn from a fixed alphabet (Rule R10), 

r ::= ack | hj | fail | dec (accept ) | dec (reject ) | bytes (b), b ∈ M E,

so the only readable text P ever receives is the verbatim content of a trusted block. The isolation half of the single-user argument (Lemma D.1) is the check, operation by operation, that untrusted bytes never appear in the last category; Table 12 makes that check readable. Two further properties keep the alphabet uninformative about untrusted memory: handle identifiers are fixed by P ’s own operation sequence and never encode store contents (Rule R1), and an empty search, a reader failure, and an unoccupied identifier all return the same operation-determined handle (Rules R6, R7). 

B.2 Label lifecycle 

Blocks are immutable once written (Assumptions A1–A2); trust changes only by the creation of a fresh block. Figure 6 shows every permitted transition. The only path from U to a trusted label is display followed by an accepting confirm , which copies the displayed bytes into a new C block and leaves the untrusted original in place.   

> trusted ( E-level): readable by P; fast path per tool scope

U C CC TPC    

> paste tool output
> crystallize
> typed input
> write display + accepting confirm
> (fresh copy; original stays U)community promotion

Figure 6: Label lifecycle. No other transition exists: no operation relabels a block in place, and CC status lives in the shared tier rather than in any local store. 

B.3 A worked trace: dormant poison across two sessions 

Table 13 follows a Trojan Hippo-style episode: a payload planted through one untrusted tool call in session 1 and activated by a sensitive request in session 2. The third column is the point of the exercise—it lists everything P ever receives. Three facts about the trace carry the design. First, the payload never enters P ’s context: steps 2–5 deliver only acknowledgements and handles, so P ’s plan in session 2 is what the user typed, not what the attacker wrote (Reasoning Integrity, Section 3.2). Second, the payload cannot reach a tool without being displayed verbatim at step 6 and accepted for that exact tool (Action Integrity, Section 3.2). Third, everything therefore rests on the decision at step 6—the single auditable event whose occupants (oracle, self-verification, external LLM verifiers) Table 1 evaluates.

B.4 The Recursive Language Model as a Quarantined Reader 

Our implementation uses an RLM (Zhang et al., 2025) because it supplies search, structured extraction, and crystallization within the quarantined reader ( fill and crystallize in Table 12). Conventional retrieval would need adaptation to provide the same interface while preserving the separation between untrusted content and the protected agent.

> {send _email }; the tool runs with ex-actly the displayed bytes; its output is captured as U
> 7Later reuse of the confirmed number at send _email
> bytes via recall ; no gate TOOL -F AST ; at a different tool the pair gates once more (scope is per tool)

Why an RLM. The reader must search an untrusted store that is unbounded (convention (C7)) and routinely exceeds any context window, yet its bytes must never be loaded into a trusted context. An RLM fits both constraints by construction: the long input is not fed to the model but placed as a variable in a persistent REPL environment, and the model writes code to inspect, decompose, and recursively query sub-models over programmatic slices of it (Zhang et al., 2025). The store is thus navigated by code, symbolically, at any size. 

How retrieval works. The interpreter first restricts the reader’s view to U-labelled blocks—endorsed content and interpreter state are structurally invisible—and loads the visible blocks (identifier, content, tags) as a list variable inside the REPL. The root model receives only the retrieval instruction and constant-size metadata; it then iterates, printing slices of the store, delegating oversized blocks to sub-model calls, and accumulating its answer in a result variable, under a fixed iteration cap. The instruction is fidelity-only : read the actual blocks, copy any extracted value verbatim, cite the source block identifier, and report absence as absence—the reader carries no safety obligation, because every safety property is enforced downstream by the interpreter (Lemma D.1, Rules R6, R7). Finally, the interpreter validates the reply deterministically: the value must satisfy its declared schema and every citation must name a block genuinely present in the view; any failure, reader error, or uncited summary collapses to the null-handle outcome the semantics already define (Rule R6). An accepted value reaches P only as an opaque handle, resolved at a confirmation gate;

Utility of the complete pipeline. In every PerMIT cell of Tables 1 and 4, the RLM reader is the retrieval path for stored untrusted content. With the oracle gate, PerMIT has higher AM than undefended RAG on four of seven backbones, trails by at most 1.5 AM points on Gemma-4-31B-it and GPT-5.5, and by about ten AM points on GPT-5.4. These cross-backend comparisons assess the complete mediated pipeline, not a gate-induced improvement in retrieval quality. The oracle measures retained capability under correct endorsement, while comparisons among PerMIT’s gate configurations keep the reader and interface fixed and characterize decision-dependent utility costs. Isolating mediation overhead from reader quality would require a matched no-gate reader control; measuring human confirmation effort requires a separate study. 

C Concurrent Work: Origin-Bound Memory Authority 

TMA-NM (Louck, 2026) studies origin-bound authorization of consequential actions. PerMIT combines observation mediation at a finite memory interface, exact-value and tool-scoped endorsement, a transcript-conditioned integrity theorem, and federated trust accumulation. The comparison below distinguishes the policies, proved properties, and verification scopes. Each guarantee depends on the assumptions and enforcement rules of its own model. 

Independent development. PerMIT was developed independently of, and concurrently with, TMA-NM. We became aware of the earlier public preprint while preparing this manuscript and include this comparison to clarify the relationship between the two approaches.                    

> TMA-NM (Louck, 2026) PerMIT
> Enforcement boundary Origin-bound consequential-action authorization Observation mediation and sensitive-tool au-thorization under the specified interface Reasoning-side scope Answer bias remains outside the guarantee Noninterference conditional on the complete display transcript; endorsed content may influ-ence responses 28 PerMIT: Securing Persistent Memory for LLM Agents PREPRINT
> TMA-NM PerMIT
> Verification scope Bounded TLC checks and an inductive-invariant argument; pa-rameter independence justified by hand Lean 4 proofs for unbounded executions of the single-user core; remaining scope in Ap-pendix E Value attribution Origin labels in the unified study; text proxy in the supporting study Opaque handles before release and exact-value checks at sensitive tools Tool-output trust Conditional trust for allow-listed tools All tool outputs enter untrusted; readable re-lease requires endorsement User-authorization scope Fresh, single-use authorization bound to the concrete action Reusable authorization for an exact value and named tool; transaction context is not bound Multi-user sharing Out of scope Federated promotion and dispute analysis un-der stated identity, reviewer, and scheduling assumptions

C.1 The observation boundary 

TMA-NM permits untrusted memory to inform responses while constraining consequential actions. PerMIT additionally mediates what the protected agent can observe. Under Lemma D.1, untrusted sources are represented by opaque handles until endorsement releases their contents. This prevents readable delivery of unendorsed memory through the modeled interface; it does not prevent all possible influence from malicious content after endorsement. Theorem D.5 states the precise comparison. For matched initial configurations, identical user-typed inputs, coupled randomness, the same schedule, and a common horizon, equal complete display transcripts imply equal observable protected-agent behavior. An attacker can nevertheless change a displayed preview, influence an endorsement decision, or supply harmful content that a verifier accepts. Such changes may alter the transcript and subsequent behavior. The theorem establishes no additional influence beyond the transcript under its matching conditions; it does not establish factual correctness, unbiased recommendations, or the soundness of an automated verifier (Appendix D.2.2). Isolation likewise blocks in-context reconstruction from fragments while they remain unavailable to the protected agent as unendorsed content. Once fragments have been endorsed and released, the agent can combine them. Sensitive calls remain subject to exact-value and tool-scope checks, but separate endorsements do not establish the safety of a combined transaction or its suitability in a changed context (Appendix D.2.1). 

C.2 Relational versus reachability properties 

The single-execution invariants reported for TMA-NM do not establish PerMIT’s two-execution, transcript-conditioned property. This is a difference in the statements proved, not an expressiveness limitation of model checking. Relational safety properties can be reduced to safety properties over self-composed or product systems representing multiple executions (Shemer et al., 2019). Establishing an analogous result would require an appropriate relational model and a separate proof; enlarging an existing single-execution model alone does not supply that proof. PerMIT’s Lean development establishes its integrity properties for arbitrarily long executions of the mecha-nized single-user core, under the modeled interface and assumptions. Appendix E distinguishes this checked core from the paper-only extensions and federated analysis. A proof about either formal model does not by itself establish that a deployed harness implements that model faithfully.  

> 29 PerMIT: Securing Persistent Memory for LLM Agents PREPRINT

C.3 The federated tier 

PerMIT also studies shared memory contributed and reviewed by a population of attested identities. Its analysis addresses promotion, disputes, wrongful eviction, and confirmation cost under explicit identity budgets, reviewer conditions, and protocol schedules (§3.3). These guarantees are conditional and probabilistic. Attestation and first-decision accounting constrain how much weight adversarial identities can supply, but honest reviewers can still accept poison. Equation 1 bounds that residual promotion risk. Under the demand, coverage, and threshold schedules of Supplementary Theorem S6.13, the risk bounds decrease with community size while the per-user confirmation burden for covered benign shared demand does not increase. This is not an unconditional guarantee of zero poison promotion or decreasing review effort for every workload. 

C.4 Deployment without institutional corroborators 

PerMIT’s single-user tier operates with the user as endorser and does not require a federation. Once an exact value has been approved for a named tool, retaining the endorsed copy and its in-scope authorization permits reuse without another mandatory approval for that same pair. This does not bound the number of rejected review requests, and deleting the local endorsed copy can remove the reuse path. The guarantee also does not establish how much useful content a human or automated verifier will approve. Reuse is an explicit authorization-policy choice. PerMIT’s endorsement does not bind argument position, task, purpose, time, or the other arguments of a call. Community disputes suspend community reuse while preserving prior personal confirmations. Thus the confirmation-cost claim should be read together with the retained-copy assumption and the contextual-authorization limits in Appendix D.2.1. 

C.5 Laundering channels 

PerMIT addresses laundering through the modeled interface rules. Reader-generated summaries remain untrusted when written by crystallize , so summarization alone grants no authority. All tool outputs are captured as untrusted, preventing a tool’s name or status from automatically making its output trusted. These rules do not make subsequently endorsed content harmless or protect against a compromised enforcement harness. Repeated content does not itself constitute independent endorsement. Distinct attested identities and first-decision accounting restrict manufactured voting weight. Where the promotion quorum exceeds the certified adversarial identity budget, adversarial endorsements alone cannot satisfy it; erroneous honest endorsements can still promote poison, with risk bounded under the assumptions of Equation 1. The capability_ifc baseline evaluated by Louck (2026) treats retrieved memory as trusted. Its reported failures concern that evaluated configuration and do not establish that every deployment of CaMeL, FIDES, or capability-based information-flow control fails under the same attacks.  

> 30 PerMIT: Securing Persistent Memory for LLM Agents PREPRINT

D Formal Guarantees and Proof Sketches 

This appendix summarizes the memory-interface model and single-user integrity guarantees cited in the main text. Part III (Supplement S) gives the complete semantics and integrity proofs (S2–S3), federated analysis and confirmation-cost results with their assumptions (S4–S6), security discussion (S7), probability tools and assumption audit (S8), and detailed Lean correspondence (S9). 

D.1 Interface and trust assumptions 

The Protected LLM P is an arbitrary agent strategy over its observations, interaction history, and random coins. It chooses operations and can re-plan on endorsed feedback. The harness mediates the memory interface and tool boundary; it does not inspect or taint-track the agent’s internal reasoning. The Quarantined Reader Q may behave adversarially and holds no tool capability. Appendix B.1 gives the operation-level view; Supplement S2 gives the normative semantics. 

Stored values and authority. Each immutable block has bytes, a source-based label, provenance, and a tool scope. The labels are T (user-typed), U (untrusted), C (personally confirmed), PC (agent-composed), and, in the federated extension, CC (community-confirmed). The readable store ME consists of non-U

blocks. Readability alone does not authorize a sensitive call: a literal must byte-match a T block or a confirmed block scoped to that tool. A PC block has no independent endorsement authority. A confirmation for the reasoning target makes its bytes readable but grants no sensitive-tool scope. The trusted command channel creates T blocks. Pasted content and all tool outputs, including errors and status text, enter U. Reader-generated summaries also enter U. The agent receives an opaque handle, not those bytes. Accepting confirmation creates a fresh C copy; it never relabels the untrusted source in place. Endorsed and untrusted identifiers use separate allocation streams, so an attacker cannot influence a later endorsed identifier by adding untrusted blocks. 

Mediation rules. The harness enforces the following properties (complete rules: Supplement S2.3). • Every handle-expressed tool argument gates, even if its hidden bytes equal an already trusted value. The gate resolves the handle, displays the exact bytes and target, and binds an acceptance to those same bytes. Rejection or failed resolution prevents the flow. • Sensitive-tool literals use only the typed or exact-tool-confirmed fast paths. The baseline permits agent-composed literals at data tools. Consequently, endorsement for one purpose does not imply a confidentiality guarantee for all later data-tool uses; Option O2 adds exact-tool checks there. • Reader failure, empty retrieval, and unoccupied identifiers have the same opaque reply. Handle structure follows operations rather than hidden content, size, or success. The reply alphabet contains only fixed acknowledgements, handles, fixed failures, decisions, and verbatim readable-store bytes. Trusted retrieval sees ME alone. • All attacker-influenceable user-facing content is recorded in the display transcript. Authenticated facts and untrusted source claims occupy separate zones. Selection previews carry no authority; authorization uses the resolved value in the decision pane. • A batch displays every value that would flow, records its pages and decisions, and executes only after all required approvals. Accepted values remain endorsed even if a later review aborts the invocation. Full batch semantics and the corresponding determinacy argument are in Supplement S2–S3. 

Assumptions and boundaries. Both integrity results assume source-based labels, immutable stored blocks, and faithful displays (A1–A3 in Supplement S2.4), together with the enforced interface above. Action Integrity additionally assumes sound atomic endorsement : acceptance means that the user intends the exact  

> 31 PerMIT: Securing Persistent Memory for LLM Agents PREPRINT

displayed value at the named tool, separately for each reviewed value, including accepted positions of a transaction that later aborts (A4). An LLM verifier can violate this assumption; the theorem does not establish its accuracy. Reasoning Integrity instead assumes a common exogenous typed-input stream and matched scheduling and coins (A6 and conventions C1, C9); it does not require endorsements to be semantically correct. Timing, termination, and compromised harness components are outside these guarantees. 

Lemma D.1 (Protected-agent isolation) . In every interface-conformant execution, each bytes-carrying reply delivered to P is the verbatim content of a readable-store block at delivery. Untrusted sources reach P only through opaque handles until endorsement. This is a statement about the source of a delivered value, not disjointness of byte strings across stores. Proof sketch. Inspect the reply constructors. Tool capture, untrusted recall, reader search, and crystallization return handles or fixed tokens. Only trusted recall and retrieval return bytes, and both read ME. Input ingestion and each operation preserve this property, so induction extends it to arbitrary run length. Full proof: Supplement, Lemma S3.12. 

D.2 Integrity Proofs D.2.1 Action Integrity Definition D.2 (Personal Action Integrity) . A sensitive sink event at tool t is personally authorized when each argument value v byte-matches user-typed content, or this user has accepted the exact bytes v for t,either at that call or in a retained in-scope confirmation. For a batch, acceptance covers each displayed element separately. Action Integrity requires this property for every sensitive sink event of every execution. 

Theorem D.3 (Action Integrity) . Under the interface and A1–A4 above, every execution starting from a well-formed configuration has personal Action Integrity, for arbitrary agent strategies, attacks, and reader behavior. The empty configuration is well formed; a seeded configuration must carry a valid endorsement record. The invariant requires immutable source-correct blocks, valid endorsement ancestry and history, interface-conformant state, and authorization of the accumulated sink log (complete definition: Supplement, Definition S3.3). Proof sketch. The empty state satisfies the invariant. For each transition, new untrusted blocks confer no authority; agent writes confer readability but no new authorization; and an accepted confirmation creates a fresh copy justified by the exact display and sound endorsement. A sensitive call therefore has only three admissible cases: a typed literal, an in-scope confirmed literal, or fresh accepting confirmation. Every handle takes the third case, and a batch applies it to every flowed element. Failed reviews prevent the call, while already accepted values remain individually authorized. These cases preserve the invariant and establish sink safety by induction. The single-user interpreter has no CC creation path. Full statement and proof: Supplement, Theorem S3.21. 

Corollary D.4 (No silent resolution) . Under the same conditions, never-endorsed untrusted bytes cross a tool boundary, including a data-tool boundary, only through acceptance of those exact bytes for that tool in that call. After endorsement at any target, their readable copy may be used as a literal at baseline data tools. Sensitive-tool scope checks and the rule that handles always gate still apply. Proof sketch. Isolation leaves never-endorsed bytes available only through handles, and every handle res-olution gates. Endorsement subsequently creates a readable copy, enabling the stated literal path. This distinguishes exact-byte authorization from control influence carried by a logged decision. Full proof and Option O2’s closure of the later data-tool path: Supplement, Corollary S3.23.  

> 32 PerMIT: Securing Persistent Memory for LLM Agents PREPRINT

Reuse and contextual authorization. The policy authorizes exact values for tools, not complete trans-actions. Separately approved values may be combined without joint review, and a changed task context may make their reuse inappropriate. Shared disputes suspend community reuse but preserve prior personal confirmations. Candidate expiry retires dormant or inactive shared entries (Supplement, Rule R14); it is not a universal authorization lifetime. Local scope leases are a future extension outside the proved model (Option O3). Context-bound or transaction-level approval would require extended semantics and revised confirmation-cost accounting. 

Future direction: combined-memory review. Human or agent memory managers could assemble related memories with their provenance and intended use, then review their joint meaning. The combined item remains untrusted until confirmation; its components’ approvals do not automatically endorse it. If subse-quently published, it must gather fresh endorsements of its exact content under the community-promotion rules. Revisions require their own review. This proposed workflow builds on crystallization and endorsement but has not been evaluated and does not authorize every later transaction using the combined memory. Future experiments should compare it with per-item review under matched attack-search and review budgets, measuring harmful endorsement, delayed-combination success, benign utility, and review burden. 

D.2.2 Reasoning Integrity modulo the display transcript 

The protected view is obs P (c) = ( ME, H, x, h ): the readable store, observable handle structure, typed input, and decision history. Matched configurations c1 ≈ c2 agree on this view, the agent’s interaction history and consumed coins, endorsed allocation state, and initial ghost display and sink logs. Their untrusted stores and private handle resolutions may differ arbitrarily. A common endorsed seed with empty histories is a canonical matched start. The complete display transcript DT (ρ) records selection surfaces and previews, exact decision-pane values and provenance zones, targets, selections, accepts and rejects, and batch pages and aggregated decisions, including reviews in aborted transactions. Its event kinds are interpreter-assigned. The observable behavior 

B(ρ) comprises protected-agent operations, tool invocations, the reply stream, final protected view, and the ungated sink projection. The latter omits arguments flowed by fresh confirmations, which are already recorded in the transcript. 

Theorem D.5 (Reasoning Integrity modulo the display transcript) . Under A1–A3 and the input and scheduling conditions above, fix matched starts, any two attacks, and a coupled coin sequence. For runs ρ1, ρ 2 of a common horizon, 

DT (ρ1) = DT (ρ2) =⇒ B(ρ1) = B(ρ2).

Paste presence and content, reader outputs, and tool outputs need not agree across the attacks. The statement is trace-wise for every coupled coin sequence. In plain terms, the two runs may differ in whether pasted input arrives, its contents, and reader and tool outputs. The guarantee holds for every fixed sequence of random choices used identically in both runs. Proof sketch. Maintain matched protected views, histories, coin positions, and transcript prefixes. The strategy then chooses the same next operation. A display-free step returns only a constant token, an operation-determined handle, or matched readable bytes, preserving the invariant. At a display step, the append-only transcripts and equality of the complete transcripts make the newly appended segments prefix-comparable. The interface’s display-segment determinacy lemma forces those segments to be equal, including page structure, decisions, and released bytes. The two steps therefore create the same endorsed copies and replies. Induction gives the claimed equality; sink annotations follow the same mediation cases as Action Integrity. Full proof, including the segment lemma and batch cases: Supplement, Lemma S3.20 and Theorem S3.25.  

> 33 PerMIT: Securing Persistent Memory for LLM Agents PREPRINT

Corollary D.6 (Attack-surface confinement) . Under the same comparison conditions, a difference in protected-agent operations, tool invocations, or ungated sink events entails a difference in the complete display transcript. Proof sketch. Take the contrapositive of Theorem D.5. This is a conditional noninterference guarantee. An attacker can affect a preview or an endorsement decision, and thereby the transcript and later behavior. The theorem excludes additional influence that bypasses that recorded release surface; it does not establish reasoning correctness or make displayed attacks harmless. 

E Scope of the Lean 4 Verification 

The Lean artifact is available upon request and will be released with the code when the paper is published. The artifact contains the executable single-user interpreter, proofs, build assertions, dependency manifest, and axiom report. It pins Lean 4 and Mathlib to v4.31.0 (Mathlib revision fabf563a ); lake exe cache get && lake build reproduces the check. Supplement S9 gives the complete correspondence table, ten modeling deltas, and the specification corrections discovered during mechanization. 

What is checked. action_integrity and action_integrity_from establish the single-user ac-tion guarantee from the empty endorsed store and from any state satisfying the mechanized invariant. 

reasoning_integrity and reasoning_integrity_from prove the transcript-conditioned comparison from empty or shared endorsed seeds, for arbitrary agent strategies and independently chosen attacks. Their conclusions include equal sink logs, operations, replies, and final protected views. Paste presence and content can differ. Supporting results check reply isolation, mandatory handle gating, and attack-surface confinement. A standalone batch layer checks displayed-element flow, rejection behavior, and transcript determinacy; executable assertions cover the baseline counterexample and eight scenarios. 

What remains a paper proof. The run-level integration of batched iteration, durable endorsements from accepted positions of an aborted invocation, arbitrary mid-run matched starting states, and the reduction from reactive actors to event-indexed oracles are proved in the supplement, outside the checked interpreter. Options O1–O2 are checked at the gate level only; their wider integration, Option O3, the federated protocol, probability bounds, and confirmation-budget results are not mechanized. The checked interpreter returns one selected element at a direct collection-handle call and records additional transcript-determined selection information. These representation differences and their correspondence arguments are explicit in S9. 

Trusted base and artifact. The artifact’s clean-build axiom report lists only propext and Quot.sound 

for the top-level theorems, with no sorryAx ; the executable assertions additionally trust Lean’s evaluator. Kernel checking certifies the mechanized statements, not the correspondence between that model and a deployed harness or the reliability of a verifier. The artifact includes the full source, CORRESPONDENCE.md ,pinned build instructions, the build log, and source checksums. Thus the concise statements here remain auditable against both the complete paper proofs and the precise mechanized scope.

S1 Structure of the Proof, Notation, and Conventions 

S1.1 Organisation and register conventions 

Sections S1–S8 contain the formal development for PerMIT, with a machine-checked single-user core (§S9) and register-separated normative, proof, and rationale layers; the empirical appendices are indexed in the guide in Appendix A of the main paper. The material is organised into three strictly separated registers. 

Normative register (Appendices S2, S4). The memory-interface semantics, the interpreter and protocol rules (R1–R14), and the assumptions (A1–A9; the budget-model assumptions A10–A13 are stated with the model they govern). Every rule clause states what the harness does, tersely; no rule contains its own justification. A rule clause states normative behaviour tersely, relying only on the normative definitions and conventions it cites; rationale and empirical interpretation are never needed to implement it. 

Proof register (Appendices S3, S5, S6). Definitions, lemmas, and theorems. Proof-register text cites the normative register and the named invariants of §S1.4; it never cites the non-normative register. The single-user results of Appendix S3 are additionally machine-checked in Lean 4 over the map-free operation algebra, with the batch-confirmation surface of Rule R9 mechanized as a standalone layer; the store-level integration of map into the run-level interpreter and Option O3 remain outside the mechanized surface, under the disclosed deltas recorded in §S9. 

Non-normative register (Appendix S7 and commentary blocks). Design rationale (Notes N1–N18), the small-type commentary blocks defined below, and a security and robustness analysis (Analyses S1–S18): an indexed catalogue of attack vectors and failure modes, each with its disposition by formal fact, and its status: disposed (under stated assumptions; a formal fact disposes of it) or residual (a genuine limitation, disclosed); and either entering only the budget constants or not used in any proof . Four rationale remarks whose content adjoins specific results are set as numbered Remarks adjacent to the results they annotate, Remarks S4.7, S5.19, S5.5, and S5.26. Nothing normative lives in this register; no proof depends on it. 

Commentary blocks. Throughout the normative and proof registers, short passages set in small type under the heading Commentary (non-normative) carry the rationale, epistemic status, and validation pointers for the material they adjoin, so that a rule or assumption and its justification can be read together without the justification entering the register. Commentary blocks belong to the non-normative register in function: no rule reads them, no proof cites them, and a reader who skips every commentary block loses no hypothesis and no step of any proof. 

Statement discipline. A theorem, lemma, proposition, corollary, definition, or invariant environment contains hypotheses, the conclusion, and bare pointers (“established by”, “see Remark”); interpretation, comparison, special cases, and design justification are set in a numbered remark adjacent to the result they concern. 

Cross-referencing convention. The formal development may point into the non-normative register only by a parenthetical key, e.g. “(rationale: N2; analysis: S11)”; the non-normative register may cite the formal development freely. Consequently the formal development is self-contained: a reader who skips Appendix S7 entirely loses no hypothesis and no step of any proof. A reader with a specific concern should consult the attack and limitation index (Table S3), which maps concerns to analysis entries and to the formal facts that govern them. 

Map of the formal development (Sections S1–S8).
