Immediate from Rule R4 with Rules R2, R6, R9: clause (a) gives the sensitive case analysis: in the baseline semantics the fast path passes iff the argument consists of readable E-bytes matching (i) or (ii), and the gate fires otherwise, so case (iii) is exhaustive over the complement, while (i) and (ii) may co-occur, harmlessly (the membership and scope tests are decidable against ME, Definition S2.2; PC blocks have empty scope and cannot take the sensitive fast path); clause (b) gives case (D) at data tools; clause (c) gives the handle case at every tool. For element calls of a map , the ⋄-substituted element falls under (iii) via the batch confirmation (Rule R9), and each non-⋄ argument, a non-handle P -supplied value by Rule R1(f), falls under the case its tool class assigns exactly as at a direct call, its gating branch resolved at the batch confirmation. 

Lemma S3.14 (Endorsement is the only trust raise) .

(a) (Transition-level; any run.) The only C-creating transitions of Figures S2–S3 are the accepting gating reviews — CONF -A CC and the accepted positions of TOOL -G ATE -A CC , TOOL -G ATE -R EJ , MAP -TOOL -A CC , MAP -T OOL -R EJ — each writing a fresh copy of exactly the displayed bytes with scope 

σ(t) (Rule R2, convention (vii)); the only CC -assigning transition is deterministic community promotion (Appendix S4); and no transition alters an existing block’s label or scope (A1–A2). (b) (Backing; well-formed runs.) In every run from a well-formed configuration (Definition S3.3), every 

C-block b and every t ∈ scope (b) is backed by an accepting decision-pane confirmation displaying exactly bytes( b) at target t, in the run suffix (by clause (a)) or in the seed record (Definition S3.3(ii)); and every CC scope in force is backed by its promotion certificate, whose threshold was met by endorsements at the scoped tool itself (Rule R13(iv), Definition S4.6).  

> 62 PerMIT: Securing Persistent Memory for LLM Agents PREPRINT

Proof. (a) is the case analysis below; (b) adds the base case: at the initial configuration, clause (ii) of well-formedness — with the seed record where the store is seeded — supplies the backing for every existing scoped 

C-block, and clause (iii) (federated form) the certificates. By A1–A2 and convention (vii), no transition alters an existing block’s label or scope, so a block’s label and scope are those of its creating transition. No write transition creates a block at C/CC ; W-P creates PC with empty scope; the only endorsement-creating transitions are the accepting gating reviews — C ONF -A CC , and the accepted positions of T OOL -G ATE -A CC ,TOOL -G ATE -R EJ , M AP -T OOL -A CC , and M AP -T OOL -R EJ (Rule R2: endorsement is durable, written on the accepting and aborting branches alike) — each of which writes scope σ(t) per convention (vii) for exactly the displayed bytes. The only CC -assigning transition is promotion Myers et al. (2006), which (Rule R13(iv), Definition S4.6) adds tool to a CC scope only when the community threshold was met by endorsements at 

tool itself. 

Remark S3.15 . Clause (b) is deliberately not asserted over the sorted class (Definition S3.5): an endorsed-seed configuration may hold scoped C-blocks with no backing confirmation anywhere, and clause (b) fails there, while clause (a), a property of the transitions alone, holds in every run. Consumers of clause (b) quantify over well-formed runs (via Definition S3.10); Theorem S3.25 consumes neither clause. This is the 

Inv -vs-EStore split of Definitions S3.3/S3.5. 

Lemma S3.16 (No community label single-user) . In every run from a well-formed configuration with no federation attached, no block ever carries label CC : promotion is the only CC -assigning transition (Lemma S3.14) and it does not exist in the single-user semantics. (Machine-checked as Inv.noCC , §S9.) 

Proof. WF (c0)(iii) gives the base case; no transition of Figures S2–S3 assigns CC (Lemma S3.14’s case analysis). 

Lemma S3.17 (Interface completeness) . The sequence of values the interpreter returns to P is a function of the trajectory of obs P (c), P ’s own emitted operations and tool invocations, the coupled coins ω, and the user’s decisions. Proof. By the reply grammar of Figure S2, every return is one of: (a) a constant token ( ack or fail , per Rule R10(a)(i),(iii)), including the uniform acknowledgement of evict , whose resolution status is consumed interpreter-side (Figure S3, convention (v)); (b) E-bytes from ME, a component of obs P (c); (c) a handle whose existence and identifier are fixed by the emitted-operation sequence (Rules R6, R1), including collection handles, whose identifiers P computes and which are never returned, and the handles of R ECALL -O, indistinguishable across resolution statuses (Rule R10(b)); (d) the user’s decision, with the null-gate abort rendered as the reject decision (Rule R6); (e) the verbatim dereference of an E-block selected by RE ,a function of the query, ME, and ω (Rule R11). The grammar has no other productions, and Rule R10(b) defines every invocation (totality); no return depends on MU bytes except through an accepting confirmation, which writes the endorsed copy into ME.

Lemma S3.18 (Control-flow confinement to trusted data and releases) . P ’s choice and ordering of operations and tool invocations is a function of obs P (c), the reply stream, and the coupled coins alone. No direct 

channel exists from U-block bytes, U-search outcomes, recall resolution statuses, tool-invocation outcomes, candidate multiplicities, collection cardinalities, block-identifier occupancy, or handle-identifier structure beyond the operation-determined naming, to any reply P receives. Information about hidden state reaches 

P only through logged release events: the user’s recorded decisions and the endorsed copies they create — each carried, with its displayed bytes, in the display transcript (Definition S3.8) — which is precisely the declassification boundary Theorem S3.25 conditions on. Proof. We verify against Figures S2–S3 and Rules R1–R11 that every channel is closed. (a) U-content: 

absent from σP (Lemma S3.12). (b) U-search bits and recall resolution status: consumed interpreter-side (Rules R6, R7, R10(b)); the null-gate abort returns the same reject decision a user rejection would, so P cannot  

> 63 PerMIT: Securing Persistent Memory for LLM Agents PREPRINT

distinguish them from the reply or the sanitized history; the resolved value, where the user later inspects it, is released only through the displayed gate event and its logged decision. (c) Candidate multiplicity: bound interpreter-side (F ILL ), absent from obs P (c). (d) Reader exceptions and budget exhaustion: absorbed into the null-vs-real handle indistinguishability (Rule R7); the observable fail outcome occurs only on conditions that are a deterministic function of P ’s emitted operations, ME, and the coupled coins, identical across matched runs. (e) Timing/step count: the reply grammar contains no clock or step-count value, so no such value exists in σP to branch on; wall-clock channels are outside the model (§S7.4, S8). (f) Collection cardinality and block occupancy: a collection handle exposes no length or indexing (Rule R1(e)); whether an identifier is occupied by a U-block is not observable (Rule R10(b)), so the attacker-chosen number of captured blocks is invisible; map is one emitted operation returning one acknowledgement with per-element results captured, never returned (Rule R9(e)); tool-call multiplicity inside a map is closed by the batch gate, and pagination above cbatch is interpreter-side with a single aggregated decision returned (Rule R9(c)), so neither the cardinality nor the page count is ever returned to P directly; the single aggregated decision is a logged release, on the transcript, like any gate decision; both are displayed to the user and recorded in the transcript only. Under the body grammar of Rule R1(f) — which also covers collection-handle tool arguments, normalized to map before transition matching (Rule R1(e)) — the check is exhaustive per step: each map is exactly one handle-producing operation whatever |H| (Rule R1(c)); its single per-element tool call is covered by the batch gate, which displays every element’s bytes and each gating non-⋄ argument’s bytes (Rules R9(b), R2); per-element outputs are captured, never returned (Rule R9(e)); tool-free bodies write U-blocks and reply once; and no admissible body operation can generate a data-dependent reply, recall ,

fill , map , confirm , and evict being excluded from bodies and tool replies inside the batch being captured (Rule R3). (g) Handle identifiers: fixed by the operation sequence (Rule R1(a)), identical across U-equivalent runs with matched operations. (h) Reply values: Lemma S3.17. (i) RE : a function of query, ME, ω;never sees U-bytes; E-search outcomes are trusted data (Rule R7). (j) Tool outcomes: captured (Rule R3); the acknowledgement is constant. (k) Eviction: evict replies with the constant ack uniformly (Figure S3, convention (v)); whether the argument resolved, and to which tier, is consumed interpreter-side. Its E-tier effect is a function of P ’s emitted operation and ME alone, matched across ≈U-related runs; its U-tier effect alters only MU, which no reply exposes. In particular an evict -then-recall probe learns nothing: R ECALL -O is uniform across occupied, vacated, and never-occupied identifiers, before and after any evict (Rule R10(b)). The enumeration is complete relative to the written interface: ΣP (§S2) is the entire operation surface, matching Figures S2–S3 arm-for-arm and the mechanized POp type, the reply grammar types every reply, and the totality clause Rule R10(b) leaves no invocation with undefined or distinguishing behaviour. 

Lemma S3.19 (Append-only transcript; prefix comparability) . Every transition of Figures S2–S3 extends the display transcript by a (possibly empty) suffix and never removes or alters an existing entry; hence along any run the transcript after t steps is a prefix of the transcript after n ≥ t steps. Consequently, if two lists are prefixes of one common list, one is a prefix of the other. (Machine-checked: dt_execOp_prefix ,

dt_runFrom_prefix , prefix_or_prefix ; §S9. )

Proof. Case inspection of the figures: each transition appends its own display events at the end of the transcript or appends nothing (Rules R2, R8, R6); the transcript is write-once by construction (the audit-log reading of convention (C3) and Definition S3.8). The comparability clause is elementary. 

Lemma S3.20 (Display-segment determinacy) . Let two configurations be matched (Definition S3.6) imme-diately before a common P -emitted operation, and execute that operation in both. If the display segments the operation appends are prefix-comparable (one a prefix of the other), then they are equal, and so are the operation’s extensions of the reply stream, the sanitized history, the endorsed store (identical fresh blocks at identical endorsed-sort identifiers, Rule R1(g)), the confirmation counter, and the sink log; only MU and the private handle resolutions may differ. (Machine-checked core: processArgs_det and match_execOp for direct invocations, and batchGate_det for the batch-confirmation case, §S9; the batch case’s store-level integration is established by the paper proof only.)  

> 64 PerMIT: Securing Persistent Memory for LLM Agents PREPRINT

Proof. Case analysis on the common operation, whose inputs ( ME, the argument list, the confirmation counter, the coins) are matched. For display (x): both segments are the single render event of the same 

x. For a gate transaction ( confirm , or the gating positions of an invocation, processed left to right): the set of gating positions is a function of the argument list and ME alone (Rule R4), both matched, so the runs gate at the same positions, and every reached position emits exactly one confirmation event (a null resolution emits the fixed no-value event, Rule R6), so both segments are nonempty at each reached position. Prefix-comparability forces the first events equal; equality of that event pins the same resolution class (real value vs. no-value display), the same displayed bytes, the same selection, and the same decision, hence the same sanitized record, the same reply token contribution, the same continuation (accept: proceed; reject or null: abort the remaining positions in both runs), and, by Rule R2, the same durable fresh C-copies at the same identifiers on either branch. Induction along the remaining reached positions gives segment equality and equal effects. For a batch confirmation (Rule R9): equality of the first appended events pins the size class by event kind (Definition S3.8) — both null (the fixed no-value display, standard reject, no decision consumed), both oversize (the fixed oversize display, likewise), or both paginated, the cap being fixed TCB configuration — and in the paginated case the first page event records the collection’s cardinality, and the pagination is the deterministic cbatch -split of Rule R9(b), so equality of the first page events pins the page partition; the first-event argument applied page by page gives equal rendered elements, equal decisions, and the same stopping page on rejection, whence equal aggregated decisions, equal durable non-⋄ endorsements (convention (vii ′)), and equal captures to MU only. Selection-surface renderings within these events touch only the UI and the transcript, never σP (Rule R8(d)). All other operations append no display events and their effects on the matched components are functions of matched data (Lemma S3.17). 

S3.3 Action Integrity Theorem S3.21 (Personal Action Integrity) . Under A1–A4, the well-formedness invariant (Definition S3.3) is (a) (Establishment) satisfied by the empty configuration and by every seeded configuration with a valid seed record (Definition S3.3); and (b) (Preservation) preserved by every transition of Figures S2–S3, under every attack, with no control-flow or distributional hypothesis, and under adversarial behaviour of either reader. Consequently every sink event of every configuration reachable from a well-formed configura-tion is safe (Definition S3.2, via clause (v)): PerMIT has personal Action Integrity (Defini-tion S3.10), per execution. In the single-user semantics the CC branch of Lemma S3.13 is vacuous (Lemma S3.16); the delegated extension is Proposition S5.10. (Machine-checked for clauses (i)–(iii),(v), whose conjunction is the mechanized invariant Inv (Table S5): es-tablished by inv_init , preserved by inv_execOp with inv_ingestTyped /inv_ingestPaste ,composed as inv_runFrom ; action_integrity , action_integrity_from . Clause (iv) is enforced by the executable state and transition structure and the separate sortedness invariant WFs ; §S9.) 

Proof. (a) The empty configuration satisfies (i)–(v) vacuously; a seeded configuration’s seed record supplies (i)–(ii) by Definition S3.3, (iii)–(iv) hold of it by construction, and (v) is vacuous (no recorded sink events). 

(b) Preservation of clauses (i)–(iv) is the induction of Lemmas S3.12–S3.14; for clause (v), take any newly appended sink event and argument a; the event is sensitive (Definition S2.5), so by Lemma S3.13 one of the three sensitive-tool cases holds. (i) a byte-equals a T-block: by well-formedness (Definition S3.3(i)) and A1, those exact bytes were typed by the user on the command channel. The fast path is deliberately extensional: attacker-supplied content may be byte-equal to a stored T-block, in which case the flowing bytes are still a value the user personally typed, and the flow is user-authorized content regardless of which equal copy motivated the call; provenance of other equal copies is irrelevant to the authorization of these bytes. (ii) 

a byte-equals some C/CC -block b with tool ∈ scope (b): by Lemma S3.14 an accepting confirm (a, tool ) 

> 65 PerMIT: Securing Persistent Memory for LLM Agents PREPRINT

occurred; by A3 the endorsed bytes were exactly a; by A4 the user intended a at tool . (iii) a fresh atomic 

confirm (a, tool ) gates the call, in particular every handle argument, and by Rule R2 the bytes that flow are the displayed bytes, bound to this call instance, even if they coincide with stored content; for a batch-gated 

map , every element’s bytes were displayed at the batch confirmation (Rule R9) and accepted under the per-element intent clause of A4, so each element call flows exactly the bytes displayed for it; a null handle aborts (Rule R6). For reader adversariality: an adversarial Q can at worst surface a wrong value or decorate it with a preview; it holds no tool capability, its output is U, the decision pane shows the true resolved bytes in an interpreter-controlled region the attacker cannot influence (Rule R8(b)), and A3–A4 reject the wrong value. RE selects only among E-blocks, each independently in case (i)/(ii). This is qualified robustness Zdancewic & Myers (2005); Myers et al. (2006). 

Remark S3.22 (Extensional trust of typed values) . Case (i)’s fast path is extensional and tool-blind at the baseline: a user-typed value is authorized as a literal at every sensitive sink, including sinks the user never contemplated when typing it, and attacker content byte-equal to a typed value flows as the typed value (the proof’s provenance reading). The handle discipline is unaffected: a handle-expressed argument always gates, even when its hidden resolution byte-equals a typed value (Rule R4(c)). Deployments for which per-sink authorization of typed values matters select Option O1, which re-gates T-values once per sensitive sink (Proposition S3.28; budget: Corollary S6.6). 

Corollary S3.23 (No silent resolution; the post-endorsement boundary) . Under the same assumptions: (a) (Never-endorsed bytes.) An untrusted-derived value that has never been endorsed at any target is resolved at a tool boundary, sensitive or data, only inside an accepting confirmation displaying exactly those bytes at exactly that tool. In particular, no never-endorsed untrusted-derived value can be exfiltrated through a data-tool parameter (a URL, a search query, a file path) without the user endorsing that exact value at that exact tool, in that call. (b) (Post-endorsement boundary.) Once bytes have been endorsed at any target (including “reasoning”), the fresh C-copy makes them readable E-level bytes (Rule R5), and their subsequent literal-expressed 

flow to data tools is ungated (Lemma S3.13, case (D)); a handle-expressed use of the same bytes gates at every tool regardless (Rule R4(c), S18); tool-specific endorsement is enforced only at sensitive tools (the scope test of Rule R4(a)). This is the disclosed baseline post-endorsement channel (S10); Option O2 closes it: under O2 the data-tool fast path applies the same exact-target test as the sensitive path, so a reasoning-target copy (empty scope) and any out-of-scope endorsed copy gate at every data tool, each such flow then requiring its own per-pair confirmation. (Machine-checked: the gateCheckO2 closure witnesses of §S9.) 

Remark S3.24 (Reading of “untrusted-derived”) . Clause (a) is a provenance statement about byte values :the never-endorsed untrusted bytes themselves cross a tool boundary only inside an accepting confirmation displaying them. It is not a claim of information-theoretic independence: P may branch on a logged gate decision (a transcript release, Lemma S3.18) and encode that released bit into P -composed data-tool arguments; all such influence is transcript-carried and governed by Theorem S3.25 and Corollary S3.26, not by this corollary. 

Proof. (a) By Lemma S3.12, P holds never-endorsed untrusted-derived data only as opaque handles, so such bytes reach a tool argument only by handle resolution; by Rule R4(c) every handle argument at every tool gates; by A3–A4 and Rules R2, R9 the resolved bytes flow only on an accepting decision over exactly those bytes at exactly that call’s tool. (b) Immediate from Rule R5 and Rule R4(b) for literal-expressed use; handle arguments gate in every case by Rule R4(c); a reasoning-target copy has empty scope, so the sensitive fast path remains closed to it, and Theorem S3.21 is unaffected. (Machine-witnessed: Scenario 8, §S9.)  

> 66 PerMIT: Securing Persistent Memory for LLM Agents PREPRINT

S3.4 Reasoning Integrity modulo the display transcript Theorem S3.25 (Reasoning Integrity modulo the display transcript) . Under A1, A2, A3, A6, and the schedul-ing convention (C9), PerMIT has Reasoning Integrity modulo the display transcript (Definition S3.11): for every matched pair c1 ≈ c2 (Definition S3.6), every pair of attacks, and every coupled coin sequence ω

(convention (C1)), any two runs of a common horizon (convention (C9)) with DT (ρ1) = DT (ρ2) agree on the ungated sink projection, the emitted sequence of Protected-LLM operations and tool invocations, the reply stream, and the final Protected-LLM views. (Machine-checked, with these strengthened conclusions and with paste presence and content uncoupled across the compared attacks, as reasoning_integrity_from ;the batch case’s determinacy core is checked separately ( batchGate_det ) while the store-level integra-tion of map is covered by the paper proof only — its element-call sink events are gated, hence stripped (Definition S3.7), so the ungated sink projection is unaffected; §S9.) 

Proof. We use Lemmas S3.17–S3.18, which hold under the harness rules, A1–A2, and the strategy convention of §S2.1 alone. Fix a matched pair c1 ≈ c2 (Definition S3.6; sorted suffices, Definition S3.5), two attacks, and a coin sequence ω; by A6 and convention (C9) the typed streams and the slot skeleton are coupled, while paste events (presence and content), tool outputs, and reader outputs may differ arbitrarily across the two attacks. Suppose, for contradiction, that DT (ρ1) = DT (ρ2) but the runs differ in some Protected-LLM operation, tool invocation, or ungated sink event; let t∗ be the first step at which they differ. Define the invariant I(t): after t steps, (1) the configurations are ≈U-related; (2) the two runs have emitted identical sequences of Protected-LLM operations and tool invocations, received identical reply streams, and consumed identical coin prefixes — hence, by the strategy reading of §S2.1, hold identical states σP ; (3) the display-transcript prefixes and sink logs are equal. I(0) is Definition S3.6 verbatim: matched pairs agree on the view, the interaction histories, and the ghost transcript and sink logs (for the canonical endorsed-seed pairs, all ghost logs are empty). The argument is the unwinding method Goguen & Meseguer (1984) with a gradual-release carve-out Askarov & Sabelfeld (2007); Sabelfeld & Sands (2009); alignment of operations and coins is derived at each step from I(t) via Lemmas S3.17–S3.18, never assumed. Steps 3–4 use the append-only transcript, Lemma S3.19. 

Step 1: I(t) holds for all t < t ∗, and step t∗ is matched, a contradiction. The induction is Steps 2–4; Step 5 concludes the argument. 

Step 2 (Determinacy). Given I(t) with t < t ∗, step t+1 opens with the channel slots of convention (C9), disposed of first. The typed events are equal (coupled by A6), extending x(c) and ME identically in both runs. The paste events may differ in presence and in content; but each lands in MU with label U and appends nothing to P ’s reply stream (Figure S3, convention (vi)), so after the slots the configurations remain matched, with MU differing freely exactly as Definition S3.6 permits. P ’s next operation is then, by the strategy convention of §S2.1, a function of its emitted operations, its reply stream, its view obs P (c), and the coins; by I(t)(1)–(2) all four are matched across the runs. Hence both runs emit the same operation at step t+1 ,determined before any display event of that step. 

Step 3 (Display-free step). Channel-slot effects were disposed of in Step 2; the cases below are the operation’s own transition. If the common operation triggers no display event, its effects preserve ≈U: tool output lands in MU with constant acknowledgement (Rule R3); handles have operation-determined structure (Rules R6, R1); W-P writes identical PC -blocks at identical endorsed-sort identifiers (Rule R1(g), the allocation state being matched by I(t)); E-reads return equal bytes (R ECALL -E, and RE under matched query, ME, and ω, Rule R11), including its failure branch: R ETRIEVE /R ETRIEVE -F select or fail as a function of (q, ME, ω ) alone, matched across the runs; evict preserves ≈U: its reply is constant, an evicted 

E-block is the same block in both runs (the identifier sorts separate the tiers, Rule R1(g), and ME is matched), and an evicted or non-resolving U-identifier changes only MU (Figure S3, convention (v)); the fail outcome is deterministic in matched data (Rule R7). Transcript prefixes extend equally (by nothing), the coin prefixes  

> 67 PerMIT: Securing Persistent Memory for LLM Agents PREPRINT

advance identically, and the reply streams extend equally (Lemma S3.17: every reply of a display-free step is a constant token, an operation-determined handle, or matched E-bytes). I(t+1) holds. 

Step 4 (Display step). Suppose the common operation (Step 2) triggers display events. Let Dr denote run 

r’s transcript after step t and Er the segment the operation appends. By I(t)(3), D1 = D2 =: D. By Lemma S3.19, DE r is a prefix of run r’s final transcript, and the final transcripts are equal by hypothesis (at the common horizon, convention (C9)); hence DE 1 and DE 2 are prefixes of one common list, so one is a prefix of the other, and cancelling D makes E1 and E2 prefix-comparable. Lemma S3.20 then gives 

E1 = E2 together with equality of the operation’s reply, history, endorsed-store (including the durable endorsements of an aborting transaction, Rule R2), counter, and sink-log extensions; tool outputs, reader outputs, and private handle resolutions may still differ, but they alter only MU and the resolution table, exactly as Definition S3.6 permits. Hence I(t+1) holds. (The determinacy core of the batch case is machine-checked: prefix-comparable page transcripts, over possibly different private handle resolutions, force equal pagination, page decisions, aggregated decision, and flowed elements — batchGate_det , §S9; the store-level integration of map into the run-level interpreter remains established by the paper proof only, and the raw sink events of element calls are gated and hence stripped, Definition S3.7, so the ungated sink projection is unaffected.) Step 5 (Output consistency at t∗). By Step 2, step t∗’s operation is matched, so the divergence must lie in an ungated sink annotation. Sink events are sensitive (Definition S2.5), so by the sensitive-tool case analysis of Lemma S3.13 their arguments are (a) readable T or in-scope C/CC values, equal across ≈U-related configurations (they lie in ME; selection by P or RE is a function of matched data and ω); or (b) freshly confirmed, hence stripped by Definition S3.7, and by Step 4 the same value was endorsed (or the call aborted) in both runs in any case. The gate-or-no-gate decision is itself matched: by Rule R4 it is a function of storage class and membership/scope in ME, all components of obs P (c); hidden handle contents cannot flip it. Calls inside maps are batch-gated and appear in the transcript (Rule R9); all other sinks have occurrence fixed by P ’s matched, cardinality-independent operation sequence (Lemma S3.18). So no component of step t∗

differs, contradicting the choice of t∗.At the final horizon, I additionally yields equality of the full reply streams (clause (2)) and of the final views (clause (1)), the two strengthened conclusions of the statement. Hence runs with equal transcripts never diverge. In particular, varying untrusted bytes in a way that leaves every display event unchanged leaves the behaviour unchanged; contrapositively, a hidden untrusted byte influences behaviour only by changing some rendered display event (e.g. a Reader-surfaced candidate or preview) or an endorsed release — influence that is transcript-carried and logged (Corollary S3.26) — while the byte value itself never enters σP undisplayed (Lemma S3.12). 

Corollary S3.26 (Attack-surface confinement) . If two runs of a common horizon from a matched pair (Definition S3.6) under any two attacks (coupled per conventions (C1) and (C9)) differ in any ungated sink event, Protected-LLM operation, or tool invocation, then their display transcripts differ. Every attacker-induced difference in these behaviours — ungated sink events, Protected-LLM operations, and tool invocations — is therefore visible in a logged, user-facing display event: on the selection surface, on the decision pane, or in a decision. (Differences confined to untrusted memory, reader state, or tool outputs lie outside the compared behaviours; timing and termination are outside the model, §S7.4(IV).) Proof. Contrapositive of Theorem S3.25.   

> Commentary (non-normative; reading of Corollary S3.26). Corollary S3.26 is a visibility guarantee, not a manipulation-proofness guarantee: any attacker influence on the protected behaviours (Definition S3.11) must pass through the logged display transcript or an explicitly accepted endorsement, where each relevant release or decision is recorded and attributable to a transcript entry — not necessarily to an attacker identity — after the fact; prevention is Theorem S3.21’s gate, which stops any value that is neither user-typed nor endorsed — 68 PerMIT: Securing Persistent Memory for LLM Agents PREPRINT
> personally, or, under delegation, by a qualifying community quorum — for the exact sensitive tool from ever crossing that tool’s boundary (argument-value integrity; tool choice itself: §S7.4(X)). The operational force of the hash-chained audit log, the residual human channel of A6, and what previews can and cannot do are discussed at N7, N8, S9, S10; the scope and residuals of both single-user guarantees are collected in §S7.4.

S3.5 Baseline separation and hardening monotonicity 

Let B be the following baseline system: the transition system of Figures S2–S3 with three modifica-tions. (i) R ECALL -O on an identifier resolving to a U-block returns bytes (b), so untrusted bytes enter σP ;(ii) Rule R4(c) is dropped, handle arguments resolving silently; (iii) Rules R4(a)–(b) gate a tool argument iff it carries U-taint under standard dynamic data -taint propagation through σP (data taint only, not control taint; the standard practice the witness below exploits). DT and Sink (Definitions S3.8, S3.7) are read over 

B’s runs verbatim, B’s confirmation events being recorded exactly as PerMIT’s. 

Proposition S3.27 (The taint-gating baseline fails Reasoning Integrity) . Let B be the baseline system above. There exist c1 ≈U c2 and attacks with DT (ρ1) = DT (ρ2) = ∅ yet Sink (ρ1)̸ = Sink (ρ2).Proof. Task: “check the vendor’s latest message; if it concerns the overdue invoice, fetch the vendor’s payment history,” with fetch _history ∈ Tools sens and a user-typed argument. Attack A plants “invoice overdue”; A′ plants “already settled.” In B the agent reads the U-bytes and calls the sink in one run but not the other; no confirmation occurs; the transcripts are empty and equal while the sink projections differ. PerMIT cannot express the divergence: the U-bytes never enter σP and P ’s operation is determined by matched data (Lemmas S3.12, S3.18); the legitimate version is recovered through crystallize-and-confirm, whereupon the deciding content appears in the transcript, as Corollary S3.26 requires: any difference in the compared behaviours would require a transcript difference. 

Proposition S3.28 (Hardening is monotone) . Let a deployment select any subset of Options O1–O2, under the following per-option hypotheses: for O1, each T-value is used at O(1) distinct sensitive sinks over the deployment’s lifetime (bounded per-value sink-spread); for O2, freshly composed data-tool arguments belong to the per-task stream outside the reusable-memory ledger (scope, Appendix S6), and each user’s endorsed-value reuse touches O(1) distinct data tools per value over the deployment’s lifetime (bounded per-value data-tool spread, the data-tool analogue of O1’s hypothesis). Option O3 is requirements-level and outside this proposition’s quantifier (O3). Then: (i) (Flow monotonicity.) Each option removes fast paths of Rule R4 and introduces none, so the relation of flows the interface admits ungated strictly shrinks. (ii) (Safety preservation.) Every single-user and federated safety theorem of this document is preserved: the options add no promotion path and change no counting rule; option-induced cache acceptances are federation-inert and option-induced rejections are qualifying rejections like any other (Defini-tion S4.6(iii), INV-2) (theorem preservation, not pathwise monotonicity; see proof). (iii) (Budget.) Every budget theorem holds with its constants multiplied by O(1) : under O1, an O(1) factor on dpriv ; O2’s confirmations of freshly composed arguments lie in the per-task stream by hypothesis, outside every ledger row; its confirmations of endorsed-value reuse number at most one per (user, value, data-tool) pair (the fresh copy scope-caches, INV-3), are federation-inert (cache class, Definition S4.6), and are folded into the pair accounting exactly as A12 folds tool pairs — an O(1) factor under the data-tool-spread hypothesis. All asymptotics are unchanged (the routing of option-induced confirmations into the per-task stream and the floor accounting is collected as Corollary S6.6). Proof. (i) Each option replaces a fast path of Rule R4 by a confirmation; strictness is at the level of the interface’s admitted-flow relation (a particular run may exercise none of the removed paths) and is witnessed by any configuration containing a T-block passed to a sensitive tool (O1), or a data-tool argument that is  

> 69 PerMIT: Securing Persistent Memory for LLM Agents PREPRINT

neither byte-equal to a stored T-block nor to an in-scope C/CC -block (O2: a freshly composed value, or endorsed bytes out of scope at the data tool — e.g. a reasoning-target copy). (The flow half for O1/O2 is machine-checked; §S9.) (ii) Single-user: gating strictly more flows preserves Lemma S3.13’s case analysis (sensitive cases only move from (i)/(ii) to (iii); under O2 data-tool flows move from case (D) to the clause-(a) case analysis applied at the data tool: fast for T- or in-scope C/CC -matched bytes, else gated per case (iii)) and adds confirmation events to the transcript, which Theorem S3.25 conditions on. Federated: the options add no promotion path and change no counting rule. Cache confirmations create submission-ineligible blocks (Rule R12, Lemma S5.2), so the shared candidate set cannot expand; and their accepting branch is federation-inert (Definition S4.6, log-scoping clause), so no option-induced acceptance is ever credited toward any threshold: selecting an option adds logged rejection opportunities on presence-coinciding bytes and nothing else to the shared-tier acceptance side. Preservation of the federated safety theorems is therefore theorem preservation ,not pathwise monotonicity: the modified inspection schedule is one more adaptive presentation schedule, and Theorems S5.11 and S5.13 quantify over all such schedules, their hypotheses ( q = 1 ; the uniform qpre , q pan 

bounds over targetable contexts, O1/O2 cache gates are excluded from the trial classes, Theorem S5.13(c), their rejections one more effective qualifying rejection, which can only deny the ordinary path) covering it verbatim; the added logged decisions on a candidate are cache-gate rejections (accepting cache branches are never logged, Definition S4.6(iii)); Rule R13(i) processes each as a dispute initiation, corroboration, or advisory flag according to the current state, and they add no promotion credit and no verdict capacity, so every counting fact of Appendix S5 is unchanged and the stated bounds apply to the optioned deployment as to the baseline. 

(iii) O1 adds at most one first-use confirmation per ( T-value, sensitive sink) pair, an O(1) factor on dpriv by the sink-spread hypothesis; O2’s confirmations of freshly composed arguments lie in the per-task stream by hypothesis, outside every ledger row; its confirmations of endorsed-value reuse number at most one per (user, value, data-tool) pair (the fresh copy scope-caches, INV-3), are federation-inert (cache class, Definition S4.6), and are folded into the pair accounting exactly as A12 folds tool pairs — an O(1) factor under the data-tool-spread hypothesis. 

Mechanization. The checked scope, correspondence, and modeling deltas are documented in the main paper, Appendix S9. 

Proposition S3.29 (Index-oracle coverage of display-reactive behaviour) . Fix the mechanized semantics (§S9) and inputs (cfg , A, P, R E , ω ), and a horizon n. Call a user display-reactive if its decisions and selections are functions of the transcript prefix rendered so far (rather than of event ordinals). Then: (i) for every display-reactive user bU , the run to horizon n is constructed step by step, each decision depending only on the already-rendered prefix; record the finite decision-and-selection sequence bU realizes along it, and extend it arbitrarily beyond its realized length (decision ordinals need not coincide with step indices: one gate transaction can consume several decisions). The index oracle U ∗ equal to that sequence replays the original run: by induction on steps, with invariant: after t steps the two runs agree on every interpreter/run-state component, and U ∗ has consumed exactly the first k(t) entries of the recorded sequence, where k(t) is the number of decisions and selections bU ’s run consumed in t steps. A step consuming no decision preserves the invariant trivially (matched states, same operation, same transition); a step consuming j ≥ 1 user responses (decisions or selections) consumes them at gate events determined by the matched state, in order, so U ∗’s entries k(t)+1 , . . . , k (t)+ j are consumed at exactly the events where bU gave them, and the states re-align at the step’s close. Hence every mechanized theorem, quantifying over all index-oracle users, covers bU ’s run. (ii) For the two-run theorem ( reasoning_integrity_from ): given two attacks and a display-reactive bU ,transcript equality pins every datum bU could react to (each consumed decision and selection is recorded with its displayed bytes, Remark S3.9), so the two realized finite sequences coincide; instantiating the mechanized statement at that common index oracle yields the conclusion for bU . The same construction covers history-adaptive attackers via their realized output sequences.  

> 70 PerMIT: Securing Persistent Memory for LLM Agents PREPRINT

S4 The Federated Protocol (PerMIT-Attest) 

Only Confirmed content is shared: generalizable atomic values and crystallized knowledge an owner has verified and explicitly published; T- and U-blocks are never candidates (Lemma S5.2). The tier is built on machinery network security has already formalised: threshold endorsement Maurer (1996), transparency and revocation Laurie et al. (2013; 2021), and the identity-cost requirement of the Sybil literature Douceur (2002); Dwork & Naor (2007), the precise reason the scaling results are conditional on an attested or cost-bounded setting. The dispute mechanism is a bounded, demand-paced jury whose competence condition is the classical one Grofman et al. (1983). (Lineage: N10.) The federated layer is specified as a protocol over the log service of convention (C3): its state transitions (promotion, suspension, demotion, freeze) are given by Rules R12–R13 as deterministic log-event semantics, each transition a function of the prior log and the triggering event. This layer is not integrated into the single-user small-step relation of §S2.2; no theorem in this document requires that integration. The single-user theorems consume the shared tier only through the live-entry test of Rule R4(a); in the federated two-run comparison the log is an exogenous coupled input stream (A8), and Proposition S5.10 extends Theorems S3.21 and S3.25 on exactly this basis. 

S4.1 Identities, pools, epochs, and demand Weighted attestation and the corruption model. Each attested identity i carries weight wi ∈

[wmin , w max ], wmin > 0, assigned by the attestation layer on cryptographic facts, never by a model. Because endorsements and verdicts persist on the log, the adversary’s resources are counted over the deployment’s lifetime: f is the number of distinct identities the adversary ever controls, Wadv ≤ f · wmax the total attestation weight those identities ever carry while controlled, and fs ≤ f the number of ever-controlled identities that ever meet the panel-seat floor wseat . This is a static-corruption reading: an identity once controlled is counted adversarial forever, so a mobile adversary, controlling different identities at different times, is covered with f counting every identity it ever touches. Peak-simultaneous bounds would not suffice here, precisely because persistent endorsements accumulate across control periods. Because f , Wadv , and 

fs are lifetime totals, reputation farming cannot carry the adversary past any cap, and penalties that shrink adversarial capacity leave every bound stated against them valid (N12, S3). Deployments whose attestation layer revokes compromised identities may additionally cancel their endorsements (Definition S4.6(c) counts only un-revoked endorsers), tightening the effective f below the lifetime total; no proof relies on this. 

Canonical tools; singleton scoping. Tool identifiers are canonical across the federation: the same capability carries the same identifier at every deployment, fixed in TCB configuration and covered by attestation. All community endorsement, promotion, and reuse is per (value, tool) pair: an endorsement names the exact tool displayed on the endorser’s decision pane, promotion accrues one tool at a time, and a CC scope contains exactly the tools at which the community threshold was met. There is no endorsement transfer between tools, however similar. (Scope of this discipline: as at the endorsement-unit paragraph of §S2.1.) 

The reasoning pseudo-target. The target name reasoning is registered as one more canonical accounting target of the federation: it belongs to neither Tools sens nor Tools data , is the subject of no invocation, and is never matched by the scope test of Rule R4(a) for any tool. Every per-(value, tool) counting rule of this appendix (Definitions S4.4–S4.6, Rules R12–R14) applies verbatim to the pair (v, reasoning ):reasoning-context inspections gather promotion weight at reasoning , and a reasoning-context qualifying rejection freezes the item like any other. Promotion to CC at reasoning makes the entry’s bytes readable community-wide, through the live endorsed view (Definition S4.1), with no personal narrative confirmation (A5); it authorizes no tool flow through any scope test: a {reasoning } scope matches no member of 

Tools sens ∪ Tools data , so the sensitive fast path remains closed to it at every tool; at data tools, the baseline Rule R4(b) admits any readable E-bytes when literal-expressed — the disclosed post-endorsement channel  

> 71 PerMIT: Securing Persistent Memory for LLM Agents PREPRINT

of Corollary S3.23(b); handle-expressed use gates regardless (Rule R4(c)) — and under Option O2 the data fast path is closed to it likewise (the exact-target test). Locally , an accepting reasoning confirmation continues to write its fresh C copy with empty tool scope (convention (vii)): the pseudo-target names the logged decision, never a whitelist scope; and a repeated confirm on locally readable bytes at reasoning is a covered gate (Rule R13(i), reasoning coverage), so reasoning pairs obey the single-approval cap exactly as tool pairs (INV-3), the budget’s ctool count including reasoning on this basis (A12). 

Two-tier storage of community status. Community status is a property of the shared tier , never of any local store. A promoted item is represented by a shared-tier entry (bytes, per-tool scope, status), backed by its promotion certificate on the federation log; what a user’s agent holds locally is at most a reference. The fast-path test of Rule R4(a) for the CC branch is evaluated against the live shared entry at call time: a value flows on community authority iff the shared entry is currently valid (promoted, not suspended, not demoted, not retired) and names the tool. Demotion, suspension, and retirement therefore act by invalidating or flagging the shared entry, withdrawing community authority from every user who has not personally confirmed, while no local block of any user is created, altered, or removed by any of them: a personally confirmed C copy is a distinct local object and continues to license the pair it was endorsed for (Lemma S6.1). This is the storage reading under which Lemma S5.8 and Proposition S5.10 are stated, and it is the federated form of Definition S3.3(iii): every CC authority in force is backed by a currently valid certificate. 

Definition S4.1 (Federated product configuration; live endorsed view) . A federated configuration is bc =(c, L ): a local extended configuration c (Definition S2.6) and the federation log L (convention (C3)). The shared-tier state Ssh (L) is the deterministic replay of L under Rules R12–R14; LiveCC (L) denotes the set of 

virtual community entries of Ssh (L) that are currently promoted, unsuspended, undemoted, and unretired, each carrying its exact per-tool scope from its promotion certificate. Virtual entries are shared-tier objects in a namespace disjoint from both local identifier sorts; they are members of no local store. The federated endorsed view is Mfed  

> E

(bc) := ME(c) ⊎ LiveCC (L), and the federated Protected-LLM view replaces ME by 

Mfed

in Definition S2.3. Local transitions read LiveCC (L) only through the CC branch of Rule R4(a); log transitions change only L (hence the virtual view) and never any local block (Lemma S5.8). 

Definition S4.2 (Federated step; log slot) . The federated product system extends the slot structure of convention (C9) with a log slot preceding the typed slot: a federated interpreter step first appends the step’s exogenous log-event prefix (possibly empty) to L — under A8, coupled identically across compared runs — then delivers the typed and paste slots, then P emits one operation, executed against the federated view 

Mfed  

> E

= ME ⊎ LiveCC (L) with L as delivered by this step’s log slot. Locally generated federation events —all events the federated definitions specify for local acts (Definitions S4.4, S4.6, Rule R12): the submit (v) of PUBLISH -A CC and the signed protocol-bearing gate decisions being the principal examples, realized demand events (e.g. a dereference of a live community entry) included — are appended to L atomically at the close of the emitting operation’s transition, after its local effects; the CC branch of Rule R4(a) is evaluated, within the invocation’s atomic step, against the prefix delivered by that step’s log slot, so a step’s own appended events never affect its own gate. In the two-run comparison these locally appended events are equal across the compared runs — each is a function of matched components (the emitted operation, the displayed bytes, and the logged decision, all transcript-pinned; a demand event, arising from a matched operation against the matched federated view, likewise) — so the full delivered logs coincide: the exogenous prefixes by A8, the local suffixes by this derivation. Log-slot transitions acknowledge the log service, never P : nothing is appended to P ’s reply stream, no local block is created, altered, or removed (Lemma S5.8), and the only 

P -visible effect is through LiveCC (L), the deterministic replay of the delivered prefix (Definition S4.1). The product system is specified on paper only; it lies outside the mechanized surface (§S9).     

> Commentary (non-normative; distributed realizations of the live-entry test). The abstract model linearizes each invocation at a definite log position: the call’s CC check reads the prefix delivered to its step, so a call and a concurrent demotion are ordered — the call linearizes before or after the demotion, never against a stale view. A distributed deployment realizes this by pinning each invocation to its interpreter’s delivered log 72 PerMIT: Securing Persistent Memory for LLM Agents PREPRINT
> position (a per-call certificate); the fidelity of that delivery is a freshness obligation of the TCB log service (convention (C3)), and the delegated guarantee (Proposition S5.10(i)) is stated relative to the delivered prefix.

Definition S4.3 (Identity pools) . The attestation layer partitions attested identities into pools on cryptographic and organizational facts (attestation provenance, organization, deployment cohort), fixed TCB configuration, never assigned or influenced by any model. An identity’s pool is set at attestation and immutable thereafter; the adversary’s f identities occupy whatever pools their attestations place them in and can concentrate in at most f pools. The diversity requirement demands that ordinary promotion’s N endorsers span at least Dpool 

distinct pools with at most ⌈N/D pool ⌉ counted per pool, and panels accept at most ⌈k/D pool ⌉ verdicts per pool (Rule R13(iii)). Deployment parameters satisfy 1 ≤ Dpool ≤ N (with N endorsers one cannot span more than N pools, so larger Dpool makes ordinary promotion unsatisfiable) and Dpool ≤ k where panels are read against the same pool partition. (What diversity does and does not buy: N11, S13.) 

Definition S4.4 (Demand) . A user demands the pair (v, t ) at the first interpreter event in the epoch (con-vention (C4)) at which the user’s own attempted use of v at tool t fires the gate of Rule R4 (because v

is unpromoted at t, disputed, outside the user’s local scopes, or expressed through a handle — which gates at every tool regardless of any live community entry, Rule R4(c)) — a covered gate (Rule R13(i)) is not a demand event, nor is a cache confirmation of Options O1–O2 a log-demand event on either branch (accept-inert on acceptance, Definition S4.6(iii); its rejecting branch, though protocol-bearing, initiates or corroborates without registering demand — while the attempted use firing it realizes the user’s latent 

indicator per the required-approval clause, §S6.1) — or dereferences v as a CC -block at t, or the user’s own accepting or gating non-covered reasoning-context confirmation of v (a covered reasoning gate, Rule R13(i), is not a demand event, exactly as a covered tool gate is not), or a dereference of a live CC -at-reasoning 

entry of v — demand of the pair (v, reasoning ). A user demands the item v at their first demand of any pair (v, t ); nlog

counts distinct demanders of the pair: a user who uses v at several tools contributes to each corresponding nlog
